Establishing the role of thickness in LaOBr; an alternative gate dielectric for vdW heterostructures

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Isaac Antoine Lagaud, Justin Boddison-Chouinard, Antoine Labbé, Ayse Melis Aygar, Alex Bogan, Li-Lin Tay, Takashi Taniguchi, Kenji Watanabe, Aljoscha Söll, Zdeněk Sofer, Adina Luican-Mayer, Louis Gaudreau

In electronic devices, the quality of the dielectric material is determined by figures of merit such as the band gap, leakage onset field, breakdown field and the number of built-in defects, which can all limit device performance. An additional and important metric to consider is the strength of the dielectric constant. Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is the benchmark gate dielectric for two-dimensional van der Waals heterostructures, owing to its wide bandgap, and atomically flat interface. At the same time, its dielectric constant (2–4) highlights the opportunity to explore alternative higher-permittivity van der Waals dielectrics that provide stronger electrostatic control. Previous work established lanthanum oxybromide (LaOBr), a layered dielectric with a predicted dielectric constant of 12.5, as a potential dielectric for van der Waals heterostructures. Using graphene field-effect devices with LaOBr, we show that the leakage-onset and breakdown fields decrease systematically with thickness. Dual-gate capacitance measurements combined with a dead-layer model reveal a bulk dielectric constant of $ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{bulk}} = 12.2$ , in agreement with theory, while interfacial dead-layers cause the effective dielectric constant to vary from 6 to 11 across multiple thicknesses. Benchmarked against established gate dielectrics, LaOBr combines breakdown fields comparable to hBN with dielectric constants 2 to 3 times larger. By establishing the operating regime of devices with various thicknesses, we further confirm the advantage of LaOBr over hBN for high-capacitance gate-dielectric operation. These results validate LaOBr as a promising dielectric for integration into two-dimensional heterostructures, and establish thickness-dependent benchmarking as essential for evaluating emerging layered dielectric materials.

Revealing New Leakage Paths in GAAFETs using Predictive First-Principles Simulations

New Submission | Other Condensed Matter (cond-mat.other) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Denis Mamaluy, Juan P. Mendez, Coleman Cariker, Michael Titze, Alex A. Belianinov

Gate-All-Around Field-Effect Transistors (GAAFETs) are emerging as successors to Fin Field-Effect Transistors (FinFETs) and are central to continued scaling at advanced semiconductor nodes. Despite their improved electrostatic control, recent measurements show that the drain current in GAAFETs deviates strongly from thermionic behavior at negative gate voltages, increasing as the gate voltage becomes more negative. This leakage has been attributed to direct gate-drain tunneling through the high-$ k$ dielectric, enhanced by drain-induced dielectric barrier lowering (DIDBL). Here, we use predictive, charge-self-consistent Non-Equilibrium Green Function (NEGF) simulations based on the Contact Block Reduction (CBR) method to spatially resolve this leakage pathway in GAAFETs. Unlike approaches based primarily on phenomenological leakage models, our first-principles, multi-terminal quantum-transport framework treats the source, drain, and gate as current-carrying quantum-mechanical contacts and resolves the local current density throughout the device. The spatially resolved current density shows that gate-injected carriers tunnel through the high-$ k$ dielectric preferentially near the drain side of the gate, where the dielectric barrier is most strongly lowered by the drain bias, and are collected by the drain. This provides a direct real-space picture of the DIDBL-driven gate-drain leakage mechanism.

A Solution-Processed Ti/Pt/NiOx/Ag RRAM Device with Multiple Forming-Free Non-Volatile Intermediate States

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Abhrajit Roy, Chopali Chanchal Sahu, Rudraksh Sangore, Raman Hissariya, Poulomi Chakrabarty, Harsh Roniyar, Merlyne De Souza, Debanjan Bhowmik

Artificial neural networks (ANN) implemented on crossbar arrays of non-volatile synaptic devices are known to consume much less energy compared to that implemented on conventional computing units. Solution-processed resistive random-access memory (RRAM) devices based on transition-metal oxides are suitable as such synaptic devices because of their cheap and easy fabrication method, stability, and suitable memristive properties. Here, in NiO-based RRAM devices with Ti/Pt as the bottom electrode and Ag as the top electrode (NiO layer deposited through solution processing), we experimentally report forming-free binary switching between a high resistance state (HRS) and a low resistance state (LRS) as well as forming-free pulse-driven modulation (long-term potentiation (LTP), long-term depression (LTD)) through 30 intermediate synaptic states between HRS and LRS (around 5-bit synaptic weight resolution). Many of these states are measured to be stable at least up to 1000 seconds confirming their non-volatility and their utility in storage of synaptic weights for ANN crossbar arrays. On the contrary, most prior experimental reports (including reports both on solution-processing-based and sputtering-based RRAM devices) require forming to exhibit non-volatility. Avoiding forming reduces variability, eliminates the need for high voltage pulses, and makes the crossbar array design simpler. Further, we confirm the presence of oxygen vacancies in our materials stack using X-ray photo-electron spectroscopy (XPS). Finally, we show successful data classification on MNIST and CIFAR-10 data sets using a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) model and a convolution neural network (CNN) model respectively, where the synaptic weight updates of these ANN models are calibrated against the experimentally measured LTP-LTD characteristics of the NiO RRAM devices.

Competitive scaling of small-subsystem-optimization heuristics for LABS

New Submission | Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ginger E. Lau, Adil A. Gangat

The low autocorrelation binary sequences (LABS) problem, often formulated as a spin-lattice energy-minimization problem, is a combinatorial optimization problem with a very rugged energy landscape. We demonstrate that iterative optimization of very small subsystems of n spins that are decoupled from the rest of the system achieves global optimization of LABS with a time-to-solution scaling that is competitive with heuristics that optimize the global system of N spins directly. Our isolated-subsystem heuristic with n = 4 achieves a time scaling of O(1.333N ) on the interval N = [27, 60], while the current state-of-the-art classical and quantum scalings are respectively O(1.34N ) and O(1.21N ). Our results suggest that specialized optimization hardware with only small numbers of logical spins may be of practical relevance.

Explicit Solvents Reverse Temperature Dependence of Phase Behavior in Charged Fluids

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Pierre J. Walker, Zhen-Gang Wang

In most theories of electrolyte and polyelectrolyte solutions, the solvent is treated implicitly as a dielectric continuum. While this approximation has enabled major advances, it neglects coupling between ionic and dipolar degrees of freedom. Using molecular dynamics simulations and the mean-spherical approximation, we show that this coupling qualitatively alters electrostatically driven phase behaviour. At finite ion concentrations, many-body ion-dipole correlations reverse the temperature dependence of effective electrostatic interactions, leading to upper-critical solution temperature (UCST) behaviour in direct contrast to the lower-critical solution temperature (LCST) behaviour predicted by implicit-solvent electrostatic theories. This phase reversal arises from ion-induced suppression of local dipolar screening, a collective effect inaccessible to pairwise implicit-solvent descriptions.

Direct Observation of Chirality-Dependent Berry Curvature Dipole via Cross-Axial Nonlinear Hall Effect in Trigonal Te

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jaehyun Lee, Junhee Shin, Daryll J. C. Dalayoan, Miju Park, Changhee Sohn, Gang Qiu, Noejung Park, Binghai Yan, Tony Low, Hosub Jin, Seon Namgung

Quantum geometric quantities, such as Berry curvature (BC) and quantum metric, are known to govern the physical and topological properties of materials, making access to these quantities critically important for the comprehensive understanding of the materials. Chiral crystals offer a natural platform in which structural handedness can control quantum geometry and nonlinear transport, but direct experimental access to the quantum geometric quantities has remained elusive. Here, we show that trigonal tellurium (Te) hosts an enantiomer-dependent Berry curvature dipole (BCD) that can be accessed through the measurement of cross-axial nonlinear Hall effect (NHE), unlike previously studied in-plane configurations where the BCD contribution vanishes. Angle-resolved measurements on left- and right-handed Te devices reveal a second-order transverse response whose sign reverses with crystal chirality. The extracted cross-axial nonlinear susceptibility, $ \chi_{yxz}$ , exceeds the in-plane susceptibility, $ \chi_{xxx}$ , by more than two-folds, indicating a strong BCD-driven contribution. First-principles calculations attribute this behavior to a topologically nontrivial hedgehog-like BC texture near the H points and predict a BCD that reverses sign between opposite enantiomers, consistent with experiment. These results establish cross-axial nonlinear Hall transport as a symmetry-selective probe of chirality-controlled quantum geometry in tellurium and related chiral materials.

Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall)

Atoms that Matter: Largest Materials Datasets Are 99% Redundant, and Local Chemistry Can Guide the Required 1%

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Evgeny Moerman, Igor Poltavsky, Anton Charkin-Gorbulin, Alexandre Tkatchenko

General-purpose machine-learning force fields (MLFFs) for materials are now trained on the order of $ 100$ million structures, assuming transferability requires such scale. We test this premise on OMat24, the largest available materials dataset, by training MACE models on subsets of $ 6.5$ million atoms, less than $ 1%$ of OMat24, selected in three complementary ways: at random, by latent-space clustering of a pretrained MLFF (DIRECT), and by a new model-free method maximizing the diversity of discrete, interpretable local environments (EnvTok). All three models approach the full-data reference MACE-OMAT-0 model to within a few tens of $ \mathrm{meV}/\mathring{\mathrm{A}}$ in forces, and remain close to or exceed it on formation energies, elastic moduli, heat capacities, Li$ ^{+}$ migration barriers, and molecular-dynamics stability up to $ 3000,$ K and $ 500,$ GPa. This near-independence from the selection strategy shows that chemical environment diversity, not size, defines OMat24’s information content: the remaining $ 99%$ of structures are largely redundant. Broad sampling of chemical space thus remains essential, but reference labels are needed only for structurally and chemically distinct configurations. Using atomic positions and species, optionally augmented by magnetic moments, EnvTok identifies these before any labelling or training, yielding compact, architecture-independent datasets. This hundredfold reduction makes hybrid-DFT or higher-level relabelling of general-purpose training sets feasible, and frees computational budget for extending environmental coverage and multi-fidelity learning.

Biomolecular condensates and their pathologies: A colloidal, interfacial, and transport perspective

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Vineeth Godavarti, Vinny Chandran Suja

Biomolecular condensates are micron-scale, membraneless compartments that concentrate proteins and nucleic acids by liquid-liquid phase separation (LLPS). Condensates play critical roles in transcription, mRNA metabolism, stress responses, and signaling across the cell, with their dysregulation increasingly correlated with several pathologies spanning neurodegeneration, cancer, cardiometabolic disease, and viral infections. The emergent biology of condensates can be cohesively viewed through the framework of colloidal, interfacial, and transport phenomena. Here we review the condensate literature through this joint lens, treating each condensate as a biological colloid whose behavior is captured by four measurable physical quantities: a Flory–Huggins interaction parameter that determines whether the dense phase forms at all, a partition coefficient that dictates the degree of client molecule accumulation, a viscoelastic relaxation time that sets whether the dense phase responds as a liquid or a solid, and an interfacial tension that governs droplet shape and interfacial dynamics. Each quantity is traced to its physical origin, its measured range in biological condensates, and the techniques that quantify it, for both three-dimensional droplets and two-dimensional membrane-tethered condensates. Interpreting disease as the displacement of these physical quantities from their functional ranges, we reorganize condensate pathologies into a small set of physical failure modes — saturation concentration dysregulation, aberrant aging and liquid-to-solid transitions, disrupted partitioning, interfacial aggregation, and pathogen hijacking — and show how the same parameters become design variables for condensate-targeted drugs and engineered coacervate delivery vehicles. The review concludes with open questions at the interface of condensate biology and colloidal and interfacial science.

Pre-pairing and polarization of doped charge carriers in unconventional superconductors

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Lixin Zhang, Mengyan Nie, Yan Cheng, Ran Zhao

Based on first principles calculations, we show in hole-doped La2-xSrxCuO4, the Sr dopants prefer to form dimers, rather than be isolated as generally believed. Same trend applies to other superconductors such as Na-doped Ca2CuO2Cl2, and electron type Nd2-xCexCuO4. The dimer structure makes the two doped holes (electrons) bound up and form a singlet which is also polarized. Another type of dopant in cuprates is oxygen which behaves similarly. So, we propose that, for all oxide high TC superconductors, the holes (electrons) are pre-paired (Z-pairs) during the preparation of the superconductor. The spontaneously formed Z-pairs are conceptually different from not only the Cooper pairs, but also the Zhang-Rice singlets. The attraction force in a Z-pair originates basically from the strong antiferromagnetic exchange energy. We also found that the on-site Coulomb repulsion (U) is no longer a constant due to doping. Around a doping site, U can reduce its value discontinuously, which plays a key role in stabilizing the Z-pairs against isolated holes (electrons). A Z-pair is a p-wave like localized state which breaks the symmetry of the CuO2 plain and attracts each other along the direction perpendicular to the polarization. The Z-pairs may be popular in all of the unconventional superconductors and the periodic assembly can result in the emergence of coherence states and superconductivity.

Transport kinetics in excitonic Mott insulator

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

W. J. Brunner, Zhiwen Zhou, Luc M. Nguyen, C. A. Lozano, G. Mercado, L. H. Fowler-Gerace, L. V. Butov

Indirect excitons (IXs) in a periodic moiré potential in a van der Waals heterostructure form an experimental platform for exploring Bose-Hubbard physics, including the superfluid state and the Mott insulator state. The realization of these collective states requires low-disorder materials. In particular, the localization due to the Mott insulator must be distinguished from disorder-induced localization. We studied the transport kinetics of IXs in a MoSe$ _2$ /WSe$ _2$ van der Waals heterostructure with an anomalously high IX mobility, consistent with superfluidity. This high-mobility transport demonstrates that disorder-induced localization is negligible. We found a remarkable suppression of this efficient IX transport, directly indicating the Mott insulator. As the IX density increases during the excitation pulse, the IX cloud initially expands and then contracts upon entering the Mott insulator phase. The emergence of IX cloud contraction is accompanied by a peak in the IX spatial distribution at the origin. Our simulations reproduce the observed non-monotonic IX transport kinetics, including the temporal expansion and contraction of the IX cloud, as well as the peak in its spatial distribution.

Large Out-of-plane Polarization in Two-dimensional Bi2O3 Monolayers Predicted by an AI-driven Inverse-design Workflow

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Hongmei Tan, Jing Zhang, Shan Gao, Yueyu Zhang, Xiangmei Duan

Two-dimensional (2D) polar oxides with built-in electric fields offer promising opportunities for nanoscale electronics, optoelectronics, and surface charge engineering. However, their discovery remains challenging due to the enormous structural complexity of nonlayered oxide networks. Here, we combine inverse structural design with a machine-learning interatomic potential and first-principles calculations to explore 2D oxide configurations. Using Bi2O3 as a representative system, we identify two previously unreported polar monolayers with tetragonal and orthorhombic symmetries. Both structures exhibit vertically separated Bi and O sublayers, giving rise to pronounced intrinsic polarity with Berry-phase 2D polarizations of 34.12 and 37.15 pC/m, respectively. Notably, the o-Bi2O3 monolayer exhibits one of the largest reported out-of-plane polarizations among 2D oxides. Both monolayers exhibit dynamical stability, finite-temperature structural integrity, and wide band gaps (~3.8 eV). The large polarization originates from dimensionality-induced reconstruction of the nonlayered Bi-O network, which breaks local coordination symmetry and produces out-of-plane charge redistribution, while the stereochemically active Bi 6s2 lone pair further favors asymmetric Bi-O bonding. These findings suggest dimensionality-induced local-coordination symmetry breaking as a promising design motif for high-polarization 2D oxides, and demonstrate the utility of combining inverse design with machine-learning-assisted screening to explore unconventional polar phases in nonlayered oxide networks.

23 pages, 6 figures

Fluctuation-based evaluation of thermal conductivity using deviational phonon Monte Carlo simulations

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Takuma Hori, Yuzuho Oki

We develop a fluctuation based Monte Carlo method for evaluating thermal conductivity within the phonon Boltzmann transport equation (BTE) framework. Starting from the energy based variance reduced formulation of the BTE, we derive a relation between the spatial spreading of deviational phonon energy and the macroscopic heat conduction equation. The resulting formulation expresses thermal conductivity in terms of the mean square displacement of deviational phonon particles, without imposing thermal reservoirs. Consequently, thermal conductivity along multiple directions can be evaluated from a single simulation. The method is demonstrated for crystalline silicon at room temperature in bulk, square nanowires, and anisotropic polycrystalline nanostructures. The calculated thermal conductivities agree with theoretical predictions, including the size dependence induced by phonon boundary scattering and the mode-resolved contributions. For anisotropic polycrystalline structures, the method simultaneously resolves the directional thermal conductivities and captures their dependence on grain morphology. These results demonstrate the validity of the proposed fluctuation-based approach for evaluating thermal conductivity across different transport regimes and structural configurations.

Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci)

Soft-to-Rigid: Shape Continuation as an Alternative Search for Packing Congruent Shapes

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Yohei Nakajima

Searches for dense packings of congruent shapes move rigid pieces. Hardening starts each piece as its inscribed disk and deforms it into the target shape while the container is compressed. We ask whether it is a search worth running alongside existing ones, and test it at matched budget and comparable tuning against rigid starts, growth, simulated annealing and perturbation–compression on seven families of squares, triangles, hexagons and disks in squares, triangles and circles (188 instances), scoring each method by the lowest container size it reaches. Hardening is not a better default: tuned rigid starts reach the lowest size most often. It is a different search. On squares in a square it reaches packings the other four methods miss, including Trump’s n = 11 and Wainwright’s n = 19 in 32 runs per method, and in a targeted follow-up campaign at ten times the budget (16 runs per method) it is the only one to reach the best known packings for n = 17 and n = 26, while annealing alone reaches n = 27. In exploratory ablations, compressing disks and then switching to polygons at once, or growing rigid polygons along hardening’s area schedule, shows no detectable difference from rigid starts, and hardening’s unique packings contain more tilted pieces. The bias costs elsewhere: hardening is worse on triangles and on four families held out from all development, where the short-pilot policy we tested showed no detected advantage over always starting rigid. Shape continuation is an alternative search that reaches different packings, worth keeping beside rigid ones; we did not show that splitting a fixed budget between them pays, and we have no test that predicts where it helps. Search campaigns were planned before they ran, with amendments, exploratory analyses and post-hoc checks identified separately; every run is replayable and every number is generated from an event log.

Quantum Geometry and Pair Breaking in Intra- and Intervalley Superconductivity of Rhombohedral Graphene

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Louis Primeau, Yang Zhang, Shi-Zeng Lin

The observation of superconductivity emerging from a spin- and valley-polarized normal state in rhombohedral graphene raises a basic puzzle: intravalley Cooper pairs involve states that are not generically degenerate because of trigonal warping, and therefore lack the particle–particle nesting underlying the conventional Cooper instability. While recent model studies suggest that nontrivial band geometry can strongly promote chiral pairing, how this geometric enhancement competes with realistic band-structure-induced pair breaking remains unclear. Here we study Kohn–Luttinger superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral tetralayer graphene using a DFT-fitted multiorbital tight-binding model, retaining the full Bloch-wave-function form factors in both RPA screening and the projected Cooper interaction. We separate the superconducting instability into three contributions: the symmetry-resolved form-factor-dressed interaction, the Cooper-pair phase space determined by the symmetrized pair dispersion, and the energy mismatch generated by trigonal warping. We find that the Bloch form factors strongly favor intravalley pairing, with a leading predominantly $ p_x-ip_y$ instability and a substantially larger maximum $ T_c$ than the intervalley reference state. At the same time, trigonal-warping-induced mismatch cuts off the Cooper logarithm and confines intravalley superconductivity to a much narrower region of the phase diagram. The dominant instability occurs near pair-Lifshitz transitions and primarily in reconstructed annular or three-lobed Fermi-surface regimes. Our results show that superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene is controlled by the combined effects of quantum-geometry-dressed interactions, pair fermiology, and trigonal-warping-induced pair breaking.

31 pages, 5 figures

Exotic metallic phase in a heterotic Kondo lattice: violation of Luttinger’s theorem and absence of Kondo gap

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Hanting Zhong, Shuxiang Yang, Chao Cao, Xiao-Yong Feng, Jianhui Dai, Hai-Qing Lin

Fermi surface transformation accompanied by quantum phase transition is widely observed in correlated electron systems including heavy fermion materials and van der Waals quantum heterostructures. The unified Anderson lattice description for this phenomenon suggests that their interacting ground states in the case of electron half filling always destabilize the metallic phase in the strong Kondo-coupling regime. We examine this issue within the paramagnetic phase of a Kondo lattice system with a heterotic layered structure. In the weak Kondo-coupling regime, the system exhibits a fractional Dirac liquid involving the massless or massive Dirac fermions and a charge neutral spinon Fermi surface. In the strong Kondo-coupling regime, the spinons hybridize with the Dirac fermions resulting in an intermediate nearly-flat hybridizing band together with the conduction and valence heavy fermion bands, yielding a large electric Fermi surface. Absence of the Kondo gap between these bands is observed depending on the stacking configurations of the layered structure. In particular, the system remains metallic even at the exact half filling in this regime and Luttinger’s theorem is violated across the critical point. Implications of these results on the van der Waals quantum heterostructures are briefly discussed.

Optoelectronically Enabled Simultaneous and Decoupled Navigation and Selective Pairing of Electrically Driven Janus Particles for Multi-Agent Microrobotics

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Srikanta Debata, Ido Rachbuch, Gilad Yossifon

Self-propelled Janus particles are promising active agents for microscale manipulation and microrobotic applications. However, when powered by a global external energy source, such as an electric, magnetic, or optical field, all particles respond simultaneously, preventing their independent and concurrent control. Achieving parallel, decoupled control without continuous particle-specific feedback, while preserving intrinsic self-propulsion, remains a significant challenge. Here, we present an optoelectronic (OE) strategy for simultaneously and independently controlling electrically driven, self-propelled metallo-dielectric Janus particles (JPs) using spatially programmable virtual electrodes. These electrodes are generated by projecting a single predefined illumination pattern from a digital micromirror device onto a photoconductive surface. The resulting confinement landscape enables multiple JPs to follow distinct trajectories without continuous monitoring, feedback, or pattern reconfiguration. We demonstrate controlled pairing of JPs with passive polystyrene particles and biological cells. For automated multi-agent operation, an image-based assignment framework identifies optimal agent-target pairs, excludes intersecting trajectories, and generates a single composite pattern for simultaneous, selective pair formation without real-time updating. Finally, we demonstrate independent manipulation of multiple magnetically rolling JPs under a globally applied magnetic field. Overall, this programmable open-loop strategy provides a versatile framework for multi-agent microrobotic manipulation, with potential applications in in-vitro micromanipulation and microfluidic systems.

Advances in Complex Systems, Vol. 4, No. 4 (2001) 407-417

A Fisher-Geometric Approach to Supercell Pair Distribution Function Refinement

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Dawei Wang

Refining atomistic supercells against powder pair distribution functions (PDFs) requires optimizing many coupled atomic coordinates using a much smaller set of radial measurements. Reverse-mode differentiation makes gradients accessible for large systems, but standard first-order optimizers do not explicitly use coupled PDF sensitivities to precondition coordinate updates. We introduce a matrix-free, Fisher-geometric approach that pulls the information metric of the observation likelihood back onto atomic coordinates. The resulting update weights collective displacements according to their PDF response and experimental uncertainty. Under isotropic damping, the update is obtained by solving a damped linear system over $ M$ PDF channels, using automatic differentiation to evaluate Jacobian–vector and vector–Jacobian products without constructing the full Jacobian or coordinate curvature matrix. For Gaussian measurement errors, the update recovers classical Levenberg–Marquardt refinement while avoiding dense coordinate-space factorization. The same computational framework accommodates correlated Gaussian errors and robust likelihood-based updates. This approach combines likelihood-aware collective updates with matrix-free backpropagation for direct second-order refinement of large atomistic supercells.

Enhancing Quasi-Two-Dimensional Electronic Structure in Bi2O2Se via Chlorine Substitution

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Yong-Zhen Jiang, Qi-Xun Wen, Xiang Xu, Su-Tao Sun, Feng-Fan Ren, Zi-Xuan Zhou, Xing-Yuan You, Yu-Cheng Kan, Zheng Zhang, Jie-Yi Liu, Yu-Zhou Zhao, Zheng-Tai Liu, Mao Ye, Shu-Hua Yao, Jian Zhou, Yan-Bin Chen, Zhong-Kai Liu, Yu-Lin Chen, Yang-Yang Lv, Cheng Chen

Bismuth oxyselenide (Bi2O2Se) has emerged as a leading semiconductor candidate for next-generation electronics and quantum phenomena research. However, its strong ionic interlayer bonding of Se layers fundamentally restricts the full potential of this otherwise promising quasi-two-dimensional (2D) material. In this work, we demonstrate chlorine substitution as an effective strategy to introduce van der Waals gaps and modify the electronic structure of the system. Through comprehensive transport and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements on Bi6O6Se2Cl2 single crystal, an optimal Cl-doping compound, we show that the key electronic features, including indirect band gap, high electron mobility, and carrier effective mass, are well preserved, while the 2D character of the system is sufficiently enhanced. First-principles calculations attribute this selective tunability to the unique spatial separation between conductive Bi-O layers and dopant Se/Cl layers, which enables independent optimization of interlayer coupling and intralayer transport. Remarkably, our theoretical calculations combined with experimental observations project a broad bandgap engineering across a wide range (0.8 ~ 3.5 eV) within the Bi2O2Se system. Our findings not only expand the application potential of Bi2O2Se-based materials in optoelectronics, nanoelectronics, and fundamental research but also highlight spatial decoupling as a viable strategy for modifying detailed electronic structure of quasi-2D quantum systems.

Magnon Klein paradox

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

D. Wang

By considering the energy transport of spin wave, we show that, like the electron Klein paradox, there is no magnon Klein paradox for reflectivity. Heuristically, this can be understood according to the fact that, even if the ground state is unstable, any energy-carrying wave cannot have greater-than-one reflectivity off a passive interface, which will violate the conservation of energy. The magnon Klein paradox discussed in the literature is derived from assigning a wrong sign to the transmitted spin wave.

Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci), Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall)

Finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory: a unified framework for i-DMFT and TAO-DFT

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jingang Han, Huanjing Gong, Kai Luo, Hongming Weng, Xinguo Ren

Accurately describing chemical bond breaking requires capturing strong/static electron correlation, a major challenge for single-reference self-consistent-field methods such as Hartree–Fock and Kohn–Sham density-functional theory (DFT). Fractional occupations substantially improve this situation, as demonstrated by information-density-matrix-functional theory (i-DMFT) and thermally-assisted-occupation DFT (TAO-DFT). Although useful, they are formulated within rather different theoretical frameworks, are somewhat semi-empirical, and their relationship remains unclear. Here we introduce finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory (FT-AC-RDMFT), in which i-DMFT and TAO-DFT emerge as different reductions of a unified framework. The effective eigenvalue equation for natural orbitals and the Fermi–Dirac occupation numbers follow directly from the variational principle, while a thermal adiabatic connection relates the empirical entropy coefficient of i-DMFT to a characteristic temperature of the auxiliary non-interacting system. Within this framework, i-DMFT is the exact-exchange-based reduction, whereas TAO-DFT results when exact exchange is replaced by a density-based exchange-correlation functional. Numerical calculations confirm that i-DMFT, apart from a constant, coincides with the exact-exchange realization of the framework, and show that the improved dissociation behavior from finite-temperature occupations extends beyond exact exchange to hybrid and semilocal density functionals. For strongly correlated V2O3, finite-temperature occupations substantially reduce the severely overestimated non-magnetic LDA bulk modulus, bringing it close to experiment. These results establish FT-AC-RDMFT as a common theoretical foundation for finite-temperature occupation-based electronic-structure methods in molecular and solid-state systems.

Kink boundary-induced polarity revealed by nonreciprocal charge transport in FeTe0.7Se0.3 superconductor

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Shengyao Li, Ya Deng, Han Yan, Dianyi Hu, Zherui Yang, Xueyan Wang, Ming Yue, Qiong Wu, Zheng Liu, X. Renshaw Wang

Nonreciprocal charge transport, characterized by direction-dependent electrical response, provides a powerful probe of electronic structure and a platform for rectification. However, its realization is typically governed by intrinsic crystal symmetry, restricting the range of suitable materials. Here, we demonstrate nonreciprocal charge transport in an otherwise centrosymmetric superconductor, FeTe0.7Se0.3 (FTS), through kink boundary-induced strain engineering. The stress relaxation during growth generates a cusp-like kink-band boundary with asymmetric curvature, producing a spatially nonuniform strain field and an effective in-plane polarity that breaks inversion symmetry. Pronounced nonreciprocal transport is observed under out-of-plane magnetic fields, with strong dependence on current and temperature across the superconducting transition, in contrast to the negligible response in flat nanosheets. Our results identify kink boundary-induced symmetry breaking as a general route to engineer nonreciprocal charge transport in centrosymmetric systems.

Inverse Designing Reconfigurable Piezoelectric Tensors via Architectural and Modal Programming

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Anish Satpati, Marco Maurizi, Hyunmoon Cho, Victor Couedel, Nicolas Mule, Xiaoyu (Rayne)Zheng

Piezoelectricity arises from the coupling between electric polarization and mechanical deformation, described by a third-rank tensor whose coefficients are constrained by crystal symmetry. In natural crystals, atomic periodicity and energetic stability restrict accessible symmetries, limited to 20 distinct anisotropies that can exist. Here we establish a general framework, based on graph representation, for controlling the full effective piezoelectric tensor through architecture by resolving how macroscopic symmetry, local polarization and deformation of structural elements act together. This enables each coefficient to be traced to the members that reinforce or cancel it and be controlled through targeted geometric changes. We further uncover frequency-selective tensor reconfiguration, in which excitation frequency switches the dominant piezoelectric coefficients of a single architecture. These principles open a broader class of applications in which a single piezoelectric architecture can deliver programmable sensing, actuation and mode conversion across multiple directions and deformation states. We realize a multi-degree-of-freedom actuator that integrates distinct actuation modes in one block and reduces volume by 90% relative to conventional multi-stack assemblies.

Driven Vortex Motion in Periodic Channel Potential Embedded in Superconducting Films

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

A. Al Luhaibi, A. Glatz, Michael Vogl, Hocine Bahlouli, J. B. Ketterson

In this paper, we investigate vortex dynamics in periodically width-modulated superconducting channels by numerically solving the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equations. The channels are designed with sinusoidally varying widths characterized by their average width and modulation amplitude. The motion of driven vortices generates an oscillatory electric field with a fundamental frequency, $ f_0$ , set by the channel periodicity, together with higher-order harmonics. In contrast to the situation of vortex lattice dynamics in a periodic pinning landscape of circular holes considered by Al Luhaibi et al. [1], we show that the harmonic content of the generated electric field can be effectively controlled through the channel geometry. In particular, appropriately engineered channel profiles strongly suppress higher-order harmonics, yielding an electric-field response that closely approaches a pure sinusoidal waveform. These results demonstrate that geometric modulation offers a viable route to engineering low-harmonicity microwave signals in superconducting devices and may enable cryogenic on-chip waveform generators for controlling superconducting qubits. Such devices could provide a useful building block for scalable transmon-based quantum computing architectures.

Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con)

Active remodeling rescues a microswimmer from geometric trapping

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ayushi Bhatt, Nico Schramma, Jasper van der Gucht, Uddalok Sen

Natural habitats confront microorganisms with heterogeneous, mechanically complex surroundings that shape how they move and explore. Most studies of motility in complex media treat the environment as either a homogeneous fluid or a rigid obstacle field. Here we instead study a medium that is simultaneously soft and reconfigurable — deformable by the organism moving through it. We track the unicellular biflagellate microalga \emph{Chlamydomonas reinhardtii} swimming in a soft, disordered suspension of microgel particles comparable in size to the cell itself, across a range of gel packing fractions. With increasing packing fraction, trajectories change from long straight runs to short, frequently reorienting ones, and the effective translational diffusivity decreases monotonically. The bimodal run/tumble speed distribution, characteristic of the free-swimming gait, collapses toward a single mode as the packing fraction increases, by near-proportional slowing of both gait states rather than a redistribution of cells between them. Recasting trajectories as alternating hops and traps reveals the signature of a deformable medium: hop lengths halve as the gel densifies, approaching the microgel aggregate scale, while trapping times do not increase with increasing packing fraction. Independent force estimates place the cell’s propulsive force close to an order of magnitude above that needed to deform a single grain, so remodeling is expected rather than merely observed. A minimal \emph{active remodeler} model — an active Brownian particle among mobile, rigid obstacles — shows that obstacle mobility alone is sufficient to relocate the onset of permanent trapping. Thus, our results establish obstacle mobility as a control parameter for motility in soft, disordered matter.

Selective faceting induced by Hexagonal Boron Nitride on curved Platinum substrate

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Alaa Mohammed Idris Bakhit, Muhammad Saad Mahmood, Khadiza Ali, Anna A. Makarova, Igor Piš, Federica Bondino, Frederik Schiller, Maria Peressi

This work combines experimental investigations and first-principles calculations to study hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) growth on a curved Pt(331) substrate. Low-energy electron diffraction (LEED) and scanning tunnelling microscopy (STM) were employed to investigate the structural properties, observing that hBN growth led to faceting with several stable facets: Pt(111), Pt(221), Pt(441), Pt(881), and Pt(110). Additionally, the electronic properties were examined by X-ray photoemission spectroscopy (XPS) and near-edge X-ray absorption fine structure (NEXAFS), revealing an increase in the hBN-substrate interaction strength, from the weakest at the Pt(111) to the strongest at the Pt(110). Density functional theory (DFT) calculations confirm that the selective faceting mechanism is induced by hBN, which changes the order of relative stability of the uncovered facets. Although a complete understanding of the process is not achievable using only thermodynamical arguments, the comparison between hBN adhesion energies and clean surface free energies clearly indicates that some facets are stabilized with respect to others by stronger overlayer-substrate interaction, whereas for others the clean surface energetics plays the dominant role.

Emergent one-dimensional Bloch oscillations in coupled Ising layers

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

E. S. Ma, Z. Song

Bloch oscillations typically arise in periodic systems subjected to a constant force generated by a potential gradient. Here, we study domain-wall dynamics in coupled Ising layers under asymmetric longitudinal fields. The interlayer coupling is of Heisenberg type and acts as an effective transverse coupling in the emergent pseudospin representation. We show that the rung pseudospin structure gives rise to kinetic constraints, restricting the many-body dynamics to an emergent one-dimensional quasiparticle subspace within the original two-dimensional system. Furthermore, the imbalance between the longitudinal fields of the two layers induces an effective linear potential for the emergent domain-wall excitations, leading to their confinement between two oppositely polarized ferromagnetic domains. Remarkably, the resulting Wannier–Stark ladder of domain-wall excitations gives rise to Bloch oscillations in the absence of a conventional tilted potential, revealing a distinct mechanism for coherent quantum dynamics. Numerical simulations of time-dependent observables confirm the analytical predictions.

Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el)

A Higher-Order Stroh Framework for Weakly Nonlinear Post-Bifurcation Analysis

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Shiyuan Deng, Han Ye, Yafei Wang, Lizichen Chen, Alain Goriely, Yibin Fu, Weiqiu Chen, Chaofeng Lü, Michel Destrade, Kecheng Li, Yangkun Du

Linear stability analysis predicts the onset of wrinkling, while weakly nonlinear analysis determines whether the bifurcation is supercritical or subcritical through the sign of the cubic amplitude coefficient. When this coefficient vanishes, higher-order terms are needed. In this paper, we develop a higher-order Stroh framework for weakly nonlinear post-bifurcation analysis of finitely deformed elastic solids. For each nonzero harmonic, the same base-state Stroh operator is retained at every order, with nonlinear source terms assembled from lower-order fields to account for geometry, constitutive laws and constraints. This recursive structure, together with Fredholm solvability conditions, enables us to derive cubic, quintic and higher-order amplitude equations. Here, we demonstrate the power of this framework by analysing wrinkling in an incompressible neo-Hookean film bonded to an exponentially graded substrate. The results recover established linear instability benchmarks and the supercritical-subcritical transition in the homogeneous-substrate limit. For graded substrates, the cubic coefficient vanishes on a surface in the parameter space of modulus ratio and substrate gradation. On this surface, the quintic coefficient takes both signs, corresponding to supercritical and subcritical bifurcations. These regions are separated by a curve along which the quintic coefficient also vanishes. Determining the branch direction along this curve requires higher-order terms, which can be calculated within the same framework.

Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft)

Pressure-transferable universal machine-learning potentials: the WBM-MatPES benchmark and Tuga-FF

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Pierre-Paul De Breuck, Thalis H. B. da Silva, Antoine Loew, Silvana Botti, Miguel A. L. Marques

Universal machine-learning interatomic potentials hold the promise to replace costly ab initio calculations in materials science workflows. However, the accuracy and transferability of current models are limited by the availability of training data. We release WBM-MatPES, a dataset of 182 thousand structures recomputed with consistent MatPES settings, together with two reference convex hulls of different depths and test sets at six pressures up to 150 GPa. These datasets reproduce a realistic discovery workflow and allow potentials to be ranked systematically at this level of theory, similar to the Matbench Discovery leaderboard at the Materials Project settings. In this benchmark, five published MatPES potentials agree within 12 meV/atom at 25 GPa, yet their errors increase dramatically to 0.4-5 eV/atom at 150 GPa. To address this limitation, we develop Tuga-FF, a graph transformer trained on datasets spanning ambient and high-pressure configurations alike. Tuga-FF is the most accurate model in the benchmark at ambient pressure, with an energy error of 13.6 meV/atom, and maintains an error of just 21 meV/atom at 150 GPa. We believe this benchmark will encourage the community to train higher-accuracy models and generate richer datasets, advancing machine-learned interatomic potentials as a whole.

Spiral Edge States in Topological 2D Sb2Te3 Crystals

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Timothy Carlson, Daniel Lazega, Scott McGuire, Mihirangi Medahinne, Motahhare Mirhosseini, Gabriel Marcus, ChaoChao Dun, Maria Kolesnik-Gray, Vojislav Krstic, David Carroll

This work explores the edge state conduction properties of twinned and spiraled, 2D Sb2Te3 topological insulator crystals. We show the structure supports spatially continuous edge states within two spiral and knotted edge conduction channels. The channels are connected through a topological defect. Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci), Quantum Physics (quant-ph)

Pressure-Driven Evolution of the Physical Properties of Layered Y2GaB and Y2InB MAB Phases: A Comparative First-Principles Study

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

M. Rabi Us Sani Shapon, Md. Asif Afzal, Jubair Hossan Abir, Raihana Shams Islam, Saleh Hasan Naqib

MAB phases are nanolaminated ternary borides in which strong in-plane M-B bonds and weaker interlayer interactions drive their distinctive stability and functionality. Hydrostatic pressure offers a direct route to tune the in-plane and interlayer interactions that govern the stability and functionality of layered Y2GaB and Y2InB borides. Here, first-principles calculations are used to compare their structural, elastic, thermophysical, electronic, bonding, optical, and vibrational responses from 0 to 20 GPa. Elastic criteria show that Y2GaB is stable at 0, 5, and 10 GPa, whereas Y2InB is stable only at ambient pressure. Within the stable Y2GaB range, compression increases the elastic moduli, acoustic velocities, Debye temperature, and lattice thermal conductivity while reducing the elastic anisotropy. Both compounds are multiband metals and spin-orbit coupling modifies local dispersions without opening a gap. Y2GaB retains nine Fermi-surface sheets from 0 to 10 GPa, whereas Y2InB has eight at ambient pressure, with no pressure-induced topological reconstruction. Charge-density and overlap analyses indicate mixed ionic-covalent Y-B bonding within a delocalized metallic background. Optical parameters are anisotropic. Unit-cell phonon spectra contain positive frequencies at 0 and 10 GPa, although expanded-supercell calculations reveal low-frequency sensitivity. These results establish mechanically viable pressure windows and clarify substitution-driven structure-property relationships for these borides.

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Tharundev V V, Dushyant Singh, Vigneshswari SR, Subha Maity, Prince Rathor, Krista R Khiangte

In this paper we investigate the structural properties and thermal stability of metastable Ge1-xSnx epilayers (~4.5 at.% Sn) grown directly on Si(001) substrates by high-resolution X-ray diffraction (HRXRD) and Raman spectroscopy. Our results demonstrate that both 70 and 140 nm films are fully relaxed, with the thicker film exhibiting a small tensile strain (+0.09%). A Minimal Theoretical Model for Morphodynamic Selection in Soft Matter Systems

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Weihua Mu, Da Wang

Soft matter systems often display shape transitions, mode competition, and threshold-like dynamical responses driven by the coupling between mechanics, geometry, and biochemical or active fields. Here we present a minimal theoretical framework for describing such morphodynamic selection using a small number of physically interpretable order parameters. The model combines elastic relaxation, active driving, and nonlinear feedback, yielding distinct regimes corresponding to weak deformation, single-mode selection, and coupled shape transformation. We derive the governing equations, identify the relevant dimensionless control parameters, and show how the phase behavior can be organized in terms of threshold competition and effective coupling strengths. The framework provides a compact route for connecting analytical theory, numerical simulations, and experimentally observable shape dynamics in soft and biological matter.

Time-temperature-composition equivalence of the viscoelastic behavior through the glass transition of digitally mixed 3D-printed polymers

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Beijun Shen, Sanat K. Kumar, Mary C. Boyce

The linear viscoelastic behavior of a polymer shows an equivalence of loading frequency and temperature, the time-temperature equivalence (TTE). Horizontal shifting extends moduli measured over limited ranges to much wider ranges, through the glass transition. Multimaterial 3D printing has created digitally mixed polymers, a new class in which elastomeric and glassy resins are mixed voxel by voxel in prescribed ratios. Composition shifts the location and breadth of the glass transition and increases the rubbery modulus. Here we extend TTE to a time-temperature-composition equivalence (TTCE). Across all measured temperatures, frequencies, and compositions, the storage modulus collapses onto one master curve. Composition translates the transition and stretches its breadth, both linearly. The phase angle and relaxation spectrum superpose under the same operations, so the family shares one distribution of relaxation times. A single closed-form TTCE gives the storage and loss moduli of the family, guiding graded and architected designs for elasticity and dissipation.

EpNet-Si: Atomic Form Factors Learned from Band Structures

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Qiang Zhu

The empirical pseudopotential method (EPM) computes band structures with one diagonalization per $ k$ point and no self-consistency, but its fixed atomic form factors do not transfer between crystal structures. To address this limitation, we introduce EpNet-Si, an EPM for silicon in which an equivariant network predicts each atom’s plane-wave form factor from its local environment: a species potential plus monopole, dipole and quadrupole corrections, mixed by a learned router over atomic densities, together with an $ s/p$ nonlocal projector. With 6{,}195 parameters, the model is trained through the eigensolver on reference density functional theory (DFT) band energies of only 77 cells. Among 231 distortions of the trained structure types, the 142 gapped cells have mean band and gap errors of 15 and 8,meV; among 192 unseen structures, the 165 gapped cells have mean errors of 38 and 49,meV. Physical properties derived from EpNet-Si, including effective masses, gap pressure coefficients, deformation potentials and the density of states at the Fermi level, agree with those obtained from the reference DFT calculations. These results suggest that the classical EPM, fitted to a small set of reference data, can be extended into a fast alternative for modern electronic band-structure calculations.

DiffGA: Steering Frozen Diffusion Models for Materials Design via Evolutionary Search

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Shivang Agarwal, Rodrigo Wang, Kevin Ryczko, Aayush R. Singh

Steering candidate generation toward user-defined objectives is central to inverse materials design, yet remains difficult when objectives are black-box, changing, or non-differentiable, as in many simulation- and experiment-derived properties. Generative models offer a solution but typically require retraining or fine-tuning for each new objective, demanding labeled data and substantial compute, or rely on differentiable guidance that is brittle to gradient noise and undefined for many practical targets. We present DiffGA, a retraining-free framework that injects evolutionary search into the sampling trajectory of a retrained diffusion model, evolving partially denoised states under an arbitrary black-box oracle. Because oracle evaluation often dominates campaign cost, we couple DiffGA with an on-the-fly Bayesian surrogate that reuses representations from state-of-the-art machine-learned force fields (MLFFs). A Gaussian process is fit during the campaign on the force fields’s graph embeddings and, once it demonstrably ranks an unseen generation, replaces expensive property evaluations under an uncertainty-aware acquisition rule, reducing oracle cost with negligible loss of accuracy. Across three crystal-design tasks using a frozen MatterGen backbone, DiffGA outperforms diffusion-only, compute-matched, purely evolutionary, and fine-tuned baselines in oracle-assessed rates of stable, unique, and novel hits. Rather than proposing scientific compounds, we use density functional theory to quantify MLFF oracle fidelity, showing that it is a faithful proxy in key regimes but systematically biased in others, delineating when proxy-driven generative discovery can be trusted. On a representative sample, DiffGA’s top hits survive first principles re-evaluation as physically realistic hits, while the surrogate-accelerated pipeline preserves this fidelity at a fraction of the cost.

Role of defect trapping in the enhanced corrosion resistance of Ni-Mo alloys with yttrium and yttrium oxide additions

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jia-Hong Ke, Xinchang Zhang, Jingfan Yang, Qiufeng Yang, Ruchi Gakhar, Rongjie Song

Corrosion of structural alloys remains a critical challenge for advanced molten salt reactors, where aggressive fluoride salts promote continuous dissolution at the salt-metal interface. While most mitigation strategies emphasize thermodynamic stability, kinetic control of atomic transport has received comparatively less attention. Here, we propose a corrosion mitigation strategy based on diffusion control and vacancy trapping by dilute yttrium (Y) solutes and yttrium oxide (Y$ _2$ O$ _3$ ) dispersions in Ni alloys. This study therefore suggests point defect engineering as a promising alloy design strategy for extreme nuclear environments.

Contact-mediated asymmetric delay and rate memory in snapping beam pairs

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Gavin Linton, Shima Mahmoudirad, Nidhi Pashine, Anupam Pandey

Slender structures in dense environments interact through contact, which alters how and when they become unstable. Here we show that contact makes snap-through history dependent, so that when and whether a beam pair snaps depends on how long the contact is loaded. Under cyclic compression, the pair snaps on loading, snaps on unloading, or stays packed, with the loading and unloading rates selecting among the three. Upon contact, one beam rolls along the other without slip, and faster driving lets it roll further before it slides and snaps: to larger compression on loading and to smaller compression on unloading. This delay is asymmetric between loading and unloading as the unloading branch carries a memory of the loading rate. Interestingly, above a threshold compression the pair snaps even when the compression is stopped, with the time to snap diverging at that threshold. By exploiting this asymmetric, rate-dependent delay, we program ordered sequences of snap events in arrays of pairs with tunable gaps, and demonstrate that the energy dissipated per cycle becomes a two-parameter function of the loading and unloading rates, increasing fourfold when, at the fastest loading, the return is slowed.

Spectral Mapping Theorem between SU(N) and SU(Ns/N) Heisenberg Models and Exact Diagonalization of SU(4) and SU(7) Long-Range Ordered Phases on the Triangular Lattice

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Samuel Gozel, Pierre Nataf

We first show that, for the SU(N) Heisenberg model with isotropic two-site interactions on an arbitrary lattice of Ns sites, the full spectrum in its singlet sector (when N divides Ns) is the negative of the full spectrum in its singlet sector of the SU(Ns/N) Heisenberg model defined on the same lattice. This theorem, valid for models with the fundamental irreducible representation (irrep) at each lattice site, follows naturally from a transposition operation in the basis of Standard Young Tableaux (SYTs). Second, this basis – previously recognized as particularly convenient for exact diagonalization (ED) of SU(N) Heisenberg models – admits an efficient, memory-saving implementation, thereby enabling large-scale diagonalizations for unprecedented system sizes Ns and numbers of colors N>3. We apply these ideas to an ED study of the SU(4) and SU(7) Heisenberg models on the triangular lattice (TL), extending the calculations up to Ns=28 sites. In particular, starting from the SU(4) case, whose nearest-neighbor ground state remains debated, we confirm that next-nearest-neighbor couplings stabilize four-sublattice long-range order. We further find that increasing the number of colors from N=4 to N=7 provides another route toward a Néel-like ground state, even without such additional couplings. N = 4 to N = 7 provides another route toward a Néel-like ground state, even without such additional couplings.

Reparameterizing GFN1-xTB for Ionic Liquids

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

F. Stavarache, S. Calero, J. M. Vicent-Luna

Ionic liquids are materials whose properties can be tailored for a wide range of applications, but their chemical diversity and complex electronic structure make systematic screening using quantum-mechanical methods computationally demanding. Semiempirical quantum methods offer an alternative, although their accuracy depends on their parameterization. We investigate the reparameterization of the DFTB method GFN1-xTB for predicting structural, energetic, and electronic properties of ionic liquids. For this purpose, we built a database containing over 14,000 conformations to construct training datasets and explored different parameterization strategies fitted to binding energies, forces, HOMO-LUMO gaps, and geometries. Analysis of the original GFN1-xTB model reveals systematic errors in these properties, with distinct behavior for saturated and aromatic cations. Thus, we investigate two models: CXMIM-xTB, a targeted model trained on imidazolium-based ionic liquids, and IL-xTB, a more general model designed to describe systems containing saturated and aromatic cations. CXMIM-xTB improves the description of binding energies, geometries, and frontier orbital energies while retaining computational efficiency. The model is subsequently applied to estimate electrochemical windows of ionic liquids, providing values consistent with DFT and experimental values. IL-xTB follows a hierarchical parameterization strategy including different cation and anion families and substantially reduces the systematic errors of GFN1-xTB across the chemical space considered, providing a route for developing xTB parameterizations with broader applicability to ionic liquids. Overall, both parameterization strategies outperform the original GFN1-xTB model, demonstrating that reparameterized methods provide an efficient approach for connecting configurational sampling with quantum-mechanical properties of ionic liquids.

Nucleation at the GST–oxide interface and beam-driven ordering in amorphous Ge–Sb–Te: HAADF, EDS and electron pair distribution functions across a 4D-STEM dose series

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Mridul Kumar, Kirill Sobolev, Alexander Upcher, Yevgeny Rakita

Where crystal nuclei form in amorphous phase-change materials decides how they switch. We study six successive 4D-STEM scans, with simultaneous HAADF and EDS data, of a magnetron-sputtered Ge-Sb-Te (GST) film, restricted to the GST layer and its interface with the oxide beneath it. Nucleation sites do not differ in composition or number density: the nuclei lie on the same robust HAADF-EDS relation as the matrix, and at fixed HAADF the spread of the summed EDS signal equals its Poisson counting noise (ratio 1.03 +- 0.05). The nuclei are nevertheless crystalline and stand out in the 4D-STEM max-intensity virtual image (median z = 11.0) but not in HAADF (z = 1.3) or EDS (z = 0.3). We classify the film in four ways (HAADF intensity, I(q) phase mapping, PCA of the whole diffraction patterns and a self-supervised SimCLR encoder) and compare the electron pair distribution functions (PDFs) of all classes after calibrating reciprocal space on the Au cap. All bulk classes share one short-range structure (first shell 2.70-2.74 Å); towards the interface the first sharp diffraction peak (FSDP) moves to lower q, and every method isolates a thin interface-edge environment with a shorter or missing first shell. The I(q) classification isolates, in every scan, an interface environment that holds 62-100% of the nucleus pixels (1.2-2.5-fold above depth) and persists when the nucleus pixels are removed before classification. The interface, rather than a distinct composition or density of the nucleating volume, therefore marks where nuclei form. Over 4.7e3 e/Å^2 the HAADF signal falls by 17%, the first shell shortens by 0.03 Å and the FSDP narrows by 9%, apparently ordering the film as it damages it. All analyses used PySTEMLab, an interactive 4D-STEM analysis environment developed for this work.

Superlattice-Stabilized Fractional Chern Insulators in a “Berry Basin” Model

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Yongxin Zeng, Andrew J. Millis

The discovery of integer and fractional Chern insulators in rhombohedral graphene, following their discovery in the isolated topological flat band in twisted MoTe$ _2$ , motivates a search for the conditions under which an electronic band can host a topological correlated state. In this Letter we introduce a Berry basin" model which captures the essential features of rhombohedral graphene: a dispersive band with a flat bottom, momentum-space winding of a layer” degree of freedom, and Coulomb interactions, while also including periodic potentials, enabling controlled studies of the interplay of quantum geometry, superlattice modulation, and electron-electron interactions in producing correlated topological states beyond the isolated topological band paradigm. While integer Chern insulators emerge at strong coupling without a superlattice potential, stabilizing the fractional state requires nonzero potential for all interaction strengths we access. We provide insight into the greater robustness of integer and fractional Chern insulators in rhombohedral graphene when electrons are polarized away from the moiré interface. Our results establish a new class of systems that host robust fractional Chern insulators and provide a controlled numerical platform to test competing theories of interacting topological phases.

Local Suppression of Superconductivity in Niobium Films by Focused Silicon Ion Implantation

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Rochelle Y. Qu, Shunsuke Nishimura, Max Proepper, Joseph J. Barrera, Alexander Brooks, Joshua Vande Berg, Achim Nadzeyka, Aravind R. Sreeja, Jay C. LeFebvre, Roger K. Lake, Shane A. Cybart

Magnetic flux trapped in a superconducting circuit shifts the critical currents of nearby Josephson junctions and disturbs the operation of the circuit. The usual remedy is a moat etched through the ground plane to hold the flux away from the logic. We report direct write silicon ion implantation with a focused ion beam as a way to make moats without etching the film, by locally suppressing superconductivity in niobium (Nb). Single implanted lines were written across Nb electrodes in films of three thicknesses over two orders of magnitude in ion fluence, and the transition temperature, resistance, and critical current density of each line were measured. The transition temperature of the line falls with fluence while the surrounding Nb is unchanged, and the critical current density falls by about two orders of magnitude before the line becomes resistive at 4.2 K. The product of the critical current and the resistance of the lines follows a single power law in reduced temperature. This gives an estimate of the critical current of a line at any temperature from its transition temperature and resistance. A time dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulation indicates that an implanted region can capture and hold a vortex. From these results we suggest a starting ion energy and fluence for implanted moats in a 100 nm Nb ground plane at 4.2 K and estimate the equivalent broad beam fluence.

Transition-Metal Chalcogenide Nanowires: A First-Principles Study of Stability, Electronic Structure, and Mechanical Response

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Danilo de Araújo Moura, Flavio Bento de Oliveira, Andreia Luisa da Rosa

Subnanometer transition-metal chalcogenide nanowires provide a one-dimensional platform in which electronic functionality and mechanical response can be tuned through chemical composition. Here, density functional theory calculations are combined with machine-learning force-field (MLFF) molecular dynamics to investigate MoS, MoSe, MoTe, WS, WSe, and WTe nanowires. The optimized structures are dynamically stable within the harmonic approximation, and their electronic character evolves systematically across the chalcogen series: MoS, MoSe, WS, and WSe are metallic, whereas MoTe and WTe are semiconducting, with HSE06 band gaps of 0.52 and 0.20 eV, respectively. The effective axial stiffness decreases from sulfides to selenides and tellurides, consistent with the increase in metal–chalcogen bond length; using a circular effective cross section, the corresponding Young moduli range from 392 to 718 GPa. All six nanowires have negative formation energies relative to the selected elemental reference phases. MLFF trajectories extending to 2.5 ns at 300 K preserve the one-dimensional morphology and local atomic order, providing evidence of finite-temperature dynamical stability on the simulated time scale. The corresponding MLFF molecular-dynamics energy traces are shown in. We further examine the response of the nanowires to axial strain and curvature, including strain-induced band-gap modulation in the tellurides. These results establish composition and mechanical deformation as complementary routes for tuning the electronic and mechanical properties of ultrathin transition-metal chalcogenide nanowires and support their further investigation as components of flexible nanoelectronic systems.

Dispersion of low-energy optical excitations in NiPS$_3$ explained by singlet polaron theory

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

I. J. Hamad, C. S. Helman, A. E. Feiguin, L. O. Manuel, A. A. Aligia

Optical and resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS) experiments in the van der Waals antiferromagnet NiPS$ _3$ have revealed narrow excitonic resonances near 1.5 eV whose microscopic origin remains under debate. In this work we study the momentum dependence of these excitations using the singlet-polaron theory (SPT) previously developed for NiPS$ _3$ . Within its framework, we calculate the spectral function of the corresponding effective model and extract the dispersion of the resulting quasiparticles. The theory naturally predicts two singlet excitations whose dynamics arise from their propagation in the zig-zag antiferromagnetic background ($ S=1$ ) coupled to two-magnon processes. We show that the dispersion of the lowest-energy quasiparticle agrees very well with the excitonic dispersion measured by RIXS, reproducing both its overall shape and bandwidth along high-symmetry directions of the Brillouin zone. Our results provide a consistent interpretation of the optical excitations in terms of dispersive singlet polarons, and suggest that similar mechanisms may govern excitonic dynamics in other layered van der Waals antiferromagnets.

Framework for an AI-Ready ARPES Community Database

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Zhi-Xun Shen, Jin Gene Wong, Karen Lei, Leon Garcia, Felix Baumberger, Sergey Borisenko, Aaron Bostwick, Ting Cao, Tanny Chavez, Yulin Chen, Monika Choudhary, Andrea Damascelli, Dan Dessau, Thomas P. Devereaux, Sandy A. Ekahana, Alexei Fedorov, Atsushi Fujimori, Yucheng Guo, M. Zahid Hasan, Makoto Hashimoto, Yu He, Philip Hofmann, Zahid Hussain, Harold Y. Hwang, Zhurun Ji, Chunjing Jia, Timur K. Kim, Phil D. C. King, Takeshi Kondo, Alessandra Lanzara, Chun Lin, Donghui Lu, Matteo Michiardi, Sung-Kwan Mo, Brian Moritz, Laurenz Rettig, Eli Rotenberg, Takafumi Sato, Kyle M. Shen, Jonathan A. Sobota, Inna Vishik, Shuolong Yang, Ming Yi, Timothy Zaklama

Condensed matter stands to gain profoundly from widespread adoption of artificial intelligence (AI). However, its current means of data organization impedes this endeavor. As a premier measure of electronic structure underlying material properties, Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) has accumulated over four decades of spectra, yet the community’s publication-centered workflow preserves only a highly selected fraction of its experimental record. A shared data ecosystem could transform these fragmented measurements and their contexts into a persistent, collective scientific memory, creating immediate value for reproducibility and reuse while laying the foundation for AI-enabled discovery. In this Perspective, we report outcomes of a recent community workshop and propose a practical path toward such an ecosystem. Its benefits unfold along three timescales. In the near term, standardized, searchable, and citable datasets strengthen analysis pipelines independently of AI. In the intermediate future, community-validated tools operating across laboratories could enable broader reuse and increasingly closed-loop automated experiments. In the long term, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter. Looking forward, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter and new discoveries. We discuss lessons from existing scientific databases, the current state of AI in ARPES, as well as areas of community convergence on data organization, access, credit, governance, and a staged pilot program.

Preprint under review

Valley-resolved high harmonics probe symmetry breaking beyond the Dirac cone

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Pablo Pais, Alfredo Iorio, Marcelo F. Ciappina

Low-energy electrons in graphene obey an emergent, rotationally invariant Dirac equation, while the honeycomb lattice enters only through higher-order, symmetry-breaking corrections, the leading one being trigonal warping. This provides a unique solid-state arena to address a cornerstone challenge in quantum gravity: how to detect ultraviolet symmetry-breaking granular corrections, suppressed by a fundamental scale, atop an emergent infrared relativistic continuum. We show that intense counter-rotating $ \omega$ -$ 2\omega$ fields and valley-resolved high-harmonic generation (HHG) provide such a probe. Within a family of models that interpolates continuously between the isotropic Dirac cone and nearest-neighbour graphene at fixed Fermi velocity, the valley contrast of the harmonic yield vanishes identically in the symmetric theory, grows linearly with the warping coupling, and its valley-even counterpart appears only at second order, as expected from effective-field-theory power counting. The stronger the laser, the farther it drives electrons from the Dirac points, so the laser intensity plays the role of the collision energy. For the fourth harmonic, the leading warping correction alone accounts for the valley contrast of real graphene up to fundamental intensities of about $ 10^{11}$ W/cm$ ^2$ , below the damage threshold of monolayer graphene, whereas the fifth harmonic already reveals higher-order lattice corrections at intensities around $ 10^{10}$ W/cm$ ^2$ . The two harmonics, which rotate in opposite directions, are emitted by opposite valleys. Valley-resolved harmonics are far more sensitive than the residual valley populations, making them a practical detector of lattice-scale physics hidden beneath an emergent Dirac theory.

Three-dimensional reconstruction of weakly scattering objects from single-defocus 4D-STEM data

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Kousuke Ooe, Yufan Shen, Yuichi Shimakawa, Daisuke Kan, Scott D. Findlay

Three-dimensional (3D) atomic-structure analysis is of central importance in (scanning) transmission electron microscopy (STEM). STEM techniques to reconstruct 3D structure include tilt-series or defocus-series annular dark-field, best suited to heavier atomic species, and dynamical-scattering-model inversion applied to single-defocus four-dimensional (4D) STEM data, requiring computationally expensive post-experiment analysis. In this study, we propose a 3D-structure linear inversion (3D LINV) STEM algorithm, which enables a fast and direct reconstruction of 3D structures from a single-defocus 4D-STEM dataset. This method is based on solving linear equations relating the measured 4D-STEM data and the unknown 3D specimen potential under the thick weak phase object approximation. We show the capabilities of 3D LINV STEM through systematic 4D-STEM simulations across various observation parameters such as specimen thickness, partial coherence, and shot noise, and through an experimental demonstration of 3D structure reconstruction of a stacked freestanding membrane with atomic-scale lateral resolution and nanometre-scale depth resolution.

Weyl’s theorem and the theoretical origin of Boson peak in two-dimensional scalar spring network

New Submission | Disordered Systems and Neural Networks (cond-mat.dis-nn) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Cunyuan Jiang

The origin of the anomalous low-frequency vibrational density of states (VDOS) universally observed in amorphous solids has been debated for decades. In this work, we model amorphous solids as a zero-temperature scalar spring network, the simplest model that captures both the amorphous structure and the features of the anomalous VDOS. Using this scalar network, we mathematically demonstrate that disorder can lead to a shift of the VDOS toward low frequencies, thereby accumulating the anomalous part there. Disorder is defined through the topological connectivity of the network. The validity and universality of the theoretical framework are general and valuable for real-world scenarios because it rests only on Weyl’s theorem for Hermitian matrices and is free of perturbation and phenomenological assumptions. This work strips away complex factors and reveals, at the level of mathematical principles, a profound and clean mechanism for the origin of the anomalous VDOS in amorphous solids.
