Programmable memristive circuits for reconfigurable collective pseudomagnetic ordering, transport, and learning

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Sichang Qiu, Yuxin Jiang, Yongchao Wu, Peng Han, Xinxin Gao, Zekun Huang, Ce Shang, Ronny Thomale, Tie Jun Cui, Shuo Liu

Programmable hardware typically uses fixed architectures, whereas complex physical systems obtain diverse behavior by connecting reusable local elements through different interaction rules. Memristive devices combine writable states with nonlinear dynamics, but translating these local properties into reconfigurable collective order and symmetry-governed transport remains challenging. Here we develop modular circuits configured through programmable connections between identical memristive units. Experiments establish binary conductance states as Ising-type pseudospins and show that signed feedback between units selects uniform or staggered pseudospin order. Circuit-model simulations then examine controllable domain-wall motion and pattern recovery. A symmetry encoder combines measured conductance patterns with sublattice labels and a selected symmetry rule to configure a fixed transport layer, implementing circuit analogs of ferromagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and altermagnetic transport symmetries. Although the antiferromagnetic and altermagnetic encodings share a compensated pattern, their distinct voltage spreading illustrates how programmed circuit symmetry affects transport. Device-calibrated simulations show that memristive dynamics generates history-dependent features for reservoir computing. By separating local memory from programmable interactions, symmetry encoding and task-specific readout, this architecture provides a modular route from device dynamics to collective order, symmetry-dependent transport, and learning.

Microscopic foundation for orbitronics: orbital exchange interactions, orbital-dependent transport, and orbital switching

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Hantao Zhang, Ran Cheng

Recent experiments highlighted the essential role of the orbital degrees of freedom underlying current-induced magnetic dynamics. However, a microscopic picture of how orbital angular momenta are transferred between itinerant and localized electrons, and the way it feeds back into transport, remains elusive. Here we develop a theoretical framework for the orbital exchange interactions by generalizing the Anderson-Kondo model to incorporate the orbitals of conduction bands. Illustrating with the $ p$ -orbitals, we determine the way electrons acquire orbital polarization and its influence on the transport properties, uncovering the orbital counterparts of phenomena analogous to spin filtering and tunnel magnetoresistance. Reciprocally, we demonstrate that orbital-polarized currents can exert torques on localized magnetic moments without having to convert into spin currents, which enables electrical switching of magnetization even when spin polarization becomes inactive. These striking discoveries lay a microscopic foundation for orbitronics and reveal critical mechanisms for orbital manipulations of magnetic order.

Matrix-product-state GPLHR for non-Hermitian many-body eigenproblems

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Xingyao Wu, Zhangyu Yang, Yanbin Chen, Yu Wang, Feng Chen

Computing multiple eigenstates of non-Hermitian many-body operators plays an important role in investigating excitation spectra, spectral properties, and relaxation dynamics in interacting and open quantum systems. However, this task is challenging because the eigenvectors are generally nonorthogonal and the Hilbert space dimension grows exponentially with system size. Here, we formulate the generalized preconditioned locally harmonic residual (GPLHR) method in a matrix product state (MPS) framework to efficiently compute multiple targeted right eigenstates whose eigenvalues lie near a specified shift in the complex plane. Successive randomized compression (SRC) is employed to accelerate operator applications. We test the method on transcorrelated and complex Hubbard chains, an interacting non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain, and a Liouvillian model. The results demonstrate that GPLHR combined with MPS provides an efficient and accurate approach for computing multiple eigenstates of non-Hermitian many-body operators.

Extended-range percolation on lattices with cubic and non-compact neighborhoods in three dimensions

New Submission | Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Zhe Zhang, Yuxuan Fan, Yang Yang, Yusong Tu, Zhipeng Xun

Using MC simulations based on an efficient single-cluster growth algorithm, we investigate two types of three-dimensional extended-range percolation models, namely cubic and complex (non-compact) neighborhoods. We obtain high-precision percolation thresholds $ p_c$ for approximately 100 distinct structures and further explore the relationship between these thresholds and the topology of the lattices, which includes the total coordination number $ z$ , the number of sites $ z_i$ and these sites’ distance to the central site $ r_i$ of the given neighborhood in the $ i$ th coordination zone. Both bond and site percolation are analyzed for cubic neighborhoods on the SC lattice. For bond percolation, the asymptotic value of $ zp_c$ tends to Bethe-lattice behavior ($ zp_c\sim1$ ), and finite-$ z$ correction is found to be consistent with $ zp_{c}-1\sim a_{1}z^{-x}$ , where $ x=(d-1)/d$ . For site percolation, the value of $ zp_c$ varies non-monotonically, exhibiting an initial increase followed by a decrease with the coordination number $ z$ . Linear fitting leads to $ zp_c\sim2.5987(1)$ for large $ z$ , predicting a value of continuum percolation threshold of aligned cubes $ \eta_c=0.324838(12)$ using $ zp_c=2^{d}\eta_c$ . We then examine site percolation for non-compact neighborhoods extending to the fifth coordination zone on SC, FCC, and BCC lattices. Through a comparison of the three indices of $ z$ , $ \xi=\sum_iz_ir_i^2/i$ , and $ \zeta=\sum_iz_ir_i$ , we find that $ \zeta$ is effective in removing the degeneracy. The results obtained show power-law dependence $ p_c\propto(\zeta/K)^{-\gamma}$ with $ \gamma_{\mathrm{SC}}^{\zeta}=0.703(12)$ , $ \gamma_{\mathrm{FCC}}^{\zeta}=0.728(10)$ , and $ \gamma_{\mathrm{BCC}}^{\zeta}=0.699(12)$ for SC, FCC, and BCC lattices, and $ p_c\propto\zeta^{-\gamma}$ with $ \gamma^{\zeta}=0.719(9)$ independent of the underlying lattice. The number $ K$ indicates lattice connectivity.

Non-Hermitian Two-Kink Control of Corner States in Twisted Chern Bilayers

New Submission | Other Condensed Matter (cond-mat.other) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Xiao-Ming Zhao, Su-Peng Kou

Corner states are bound by boundary-mass defects, but this geometric anchoring restricts their spatial and internal control. Focusing on twisted bilayers assembled from Chern insulators with opposite Chern numbers, we show that a mass-phase kink carried by a non-Hermitian interlayer mass and an independent imaginary-momentum kink separate the spectral, in-plane, and interlayer control of mass-defect corner states. The mass-phase kink creates the parent corner pole and fixes its energy and intrinsic decay, while directed interlayer tunneling encodes its layer polarization. The imaginary-momentum texture instead reshapes the closed-boundary envelope and redistributes stationary right-state weight between corners. A twisted Qi–Wu–Zhang bilayer verifies this division on arbitrary, including concave, boundaries. A $ 30^\circ$ twisted Haldane dodecagon realizes twelve finite-energy parent states whose in-gap spectral manifold persists while boundary patterns redistribute its normalized right-subspace weight toward six, three, two, or one target corners. The two kinks therefore cooperate or compete while keeping spectral ancestry, spatial weight, and layer character distinct, thereby establishing a unified framework for controlling mass-defect corner states across disparate boundary geometries and mass textures.

A Solvable Model in the Half-filled Landau Level

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Anders Karlhede

A solvable microscopic model of interacting electrons in a half-filled Landau level is presented. The Hamiltonian is a well-defined limit of the electron-electron interaction. The low energy sector consists of a set of independent, spatially interlaced, one-dimensional systems of non-interacting fermionic dipoles, each system with a Fermi sea ground state. The total number of dipoles in the set of Fermi seas forming the two-dimensional ground state equals the number of electrons in the system and the density of states at the Fermi level is that of a two-dimensional Fermi gas. The model is obtained by generalizing, or rather adopting, the existing exact solution on a thin, but not too thin, torus to the two-dimensional bulk case.

Generalized Constraint-Controlled Rheological Model: Two Constitutive Pathways under Sinusoidal Periodic Shear Deformation

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Dario Nichetti, Alessio Zaccone

We formulate the Generalized Constraint-Controlled Rheological Model (GCCRM) for entangled linear and long-arm-branched polymer melts under sinusoidal periodic shear deformation (SPSD), spanning the linear and strongly nonlinear regimes. Molecular architecture, conformation, chain stretch, flow-active constraints, convective constraint release, dynamic tube dilation, and activated arm retraction define a common upstream molecular state. This state feeds two time-domain constitutive pathways (a configurational stress and a dissipative internal-variable stress) which are recombined before Fourier analysis. For branched polymers, the architecture is described by an entire backbone of molecular weight $ M_{bb}$ and $ N_a$ long arms of molecular weight $ M_a$ , with a compliance-weighted representative-constitutive-cell partition. The linear limit is normalized so that the configurational moduli sum to the entanglement modulus, while dynamic tube dilation and stretch drive distinct contributions to dissipative activation. Under reduced closures, GCCRM generates a smooth nonlinear elastic response with an onset of nonlinearity, an approximately logarithmic growth regime, an architecture-dependent maximum, and distinct high-deformation asymptotes. Fourier harmonics, ELR, VLR, and Lissajous curves are treated as post-processing observables of the total stress. The framework establishes a consistent constitutive architecture whose quantitative validation requires one parameter set per material across SPSD and, prospectively, transient shear histories.

Ergodicity breaking transition beyond qubits

New Submission | Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Tanay Pathak, Abhik Kumar Saha

The local Hilbert space dimension is a fundamental parameter in quantum many-body systems, but its role in controlling the ergodicity-breaking transition remains less explored. We systematically study this question by introducing toy models of qudits in zero dimensions that exhibit an ergodicity-breaking transition. Using the hybridization condition, we analytically obtain the dependence of the critical value of the interactions on the local Hilbert space dimension. Numerical studies of spectral correlation measures show agreement with the theoretical predictions, hence confirming the existence of the ergodicity-breaking transition in these models. We further introduce a distinct higher spin version of the model and numerically obtain the same critical behavior, demonstrating that the observed critical point is independent of the microscopic structure of interactions considered.

Unlocking the potential of polymer networks for high strength through topology

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Zheqi Chen, Yu Zhou, Chenghai Li, Ho Cheung Shum, Zhigang Suo

A covalent polymer network is orders of magnitude weaker than individual covalent bonds. This large gap reveals an unrealized potential. The weakness of a network comes not from individual covalent bonds, but from how they are connected. Here we unlock this potential by identifying a topological criterion, involving optimally sparse crosslinks, long chains, and dense entanglements. Optimizing each topological variable alone is insufficient for high strength, yet exceptional strength emerges when they are combined within a narrow regime. We describe the physics of this synergy, and use the criterion to strengthen networks widely used but known to be weak. For example, we prepare a polyacrylamide hydrogel of 90 wt% water, amplifying strength from a commonly reported value of ~0.1 MPa to ~6.5 MPa. The exceptional strength is achieved without sacrificing other properties, such as softness, toughness, and elasticity. The criterion establishes topology as a general design principle for strong polymer networks.

Dynamical chiral symmetry breaking in rotating QED$_3$: critical flavor number and the occupation-reweighting channel

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Wei Li

We study dynamical chiral symmetry breaking in $ (2{+}1)$ -dimensional QED on a rigidly rotating disc, $ \Omega R<1$ , within the rainbow Schwinger–Dyson framework. At zero temperature the rotation response of the condensate vanishes identically, so $ N_c^{\rm SD}(\Omega,T{=}0)=N_c^{\rm SD}(0)=2.60\pm0.01$ (using three operational definitions). At finite temperature, rotation suppresses the condensate through a dominant occupation-reweighting channel, $ \hat{\kappa}{\rm occ}=-1.55$ , accounting for $ \ge 99.995%$ of the linearized total response at the reference parameters, with self-consistent feedback amplifying it by $ 26.5$ . The screening channel is reported at fixed regularization, $ \hat{\kappa}{\rm scr}(\varepsilon = 0.01)=-0.97$ ; its $ \varepsilon \to 0$ behavior is logarithmically divergent. The crossover flavor $ N^{\ast}(\Omega)$ falls monotonically at fixed temperature (a corollary). The critical flavor number is unchanged by rotation at $ T=0$ (theorem). Together these results set the finite-temperature reference for the model.

Realistic Simulation of Valence-band Photoemission Spectra by using First-principles Calculations on the Basis of FLAPW Method

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Noriaki Hamada, Satoshi Nakamura, Kenta Takahashi, Mario Okawa, Tomohiko Saitoh

In order to reproduce experimental photoemission spectroscopy (PES) data by using band structure calculations with linearized augmented-plane-wave method, we developed a new method to calculate the atomic-subshell photoelectron differential cross section (PE-DCS) of the valence band. PE-DCS was calculated to be the sum of the products of the partial density of states (PDOS) and the photoionization differential cross section (PI-DCS) of each atomic subshell. To construct an appropriate PDOS beyond the conventional muffin-tin sphere (MTS) PDOS, we proposed a ``nearly-neutral atomic sphere”, which can be defined as a natural extension of MTS. Although the calculational method for atomic-subshell PI-DCS was the standard Hartree-Fock-Slater method, we developed a new computer code in order to deal with the unoccupied electron shells in the ground state of isolated atoms up to $ \sim$ 10 keV. Details of the PE-DCS calculations were examined by using two simple examples of Na metal and NaCl, and then, the method was applied to hard x-ray PES (HX-PES) data of CuAlO$ _2$ . The experimental spectra were satisfactorily reproduced, demonstrating the potential applications of our method to HX-PES spectra of many compounds.

11 pages, 8 figures

Journal of the Physical Society of Japan 93, 054709 (2024)

Single-Particle Spectroscopy and Cross-Barrier Dynamics in Inhomogeneous Luttinger Liquids via Unified Field Bosonization Technique

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Soundarya P, Venkata Suryanarayana M, Joy Prakash Das

The Unified Field Bosonization Technique (UFBT) is employed to investigate the single-particle properties of inhomogeneous Luttinger liquids containing localized clusters of impurities in the presence of forward-scattering interactions. Within this framework, fermionic two-point correlation functions are used to derive the leading-order singular contributions to the dynamical density of states (DDOS) and the cross-barrier tunneling conductance. The results reveal distinct power-law behaviours of the DDOS near the impurity cluster and in the far-field regime. The tunneling conductance exhibits a power-law temperature dependence, decreasing with decreasing temperature for repulsive interactions and increasing for attractive interactions, while remaining temperature-independent in the noninteracting limit. The impurity cluster modifies the amplitudes and spatial structure of these observables, whereas their characteristic power-law exponents are determined solely by the interaction parameters. The formulation accommodates arbitrary impurity strengths without requiring perturbative expansions in the impurity or interaction strengths. The characteristic scaling behaviour emerges directly from the fermionic correlation functions, without an explicit renormalization-group (RG) treatment of the impurity strength, while the weak-interaction limit recovers the leading perturbative correction to the tunneling response. These findings establish UFBT as a unified framework for describing local spectral properties and cross-barrier transport in inhomogeneous Luttinger liquids.

Enhancement of local magnetic ordering temperature due to non-magnetic disorder in anti-Heusler alloy Al$_2$MnCu

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Soumya Bhowmik, Suvayan Saha, Saikat Debnath, Arka Patra, Biswarup Satpati, Maxim Avdeev, Shishir Kumar Pandey, Chandan Mazumdar

Structural disorder is generally regarded as detrimental to magnetism. Since magnetic properties are governed by both the arrangement of magnetic atoms and the crystal environment, including non-magnetic atoms that mediate exchange interactions, the distinct role of magnetic and non-magnetic disorder remains less understood. The recently reported anti-Heusler alloy, Al$ 2$ MnCu, forming with $ \sim$ 50% intra-symmetry site disorder (B2) involving the magnetic Mn atom and $ \sim$ 5% inter-symmetry site disorder associated with non-magnetic Al and Cu atoms, offers a good platform to disentangle the individual roles of magnetic and non-magnetic atomic disorder. Our magnetic critical analysis, in the vicinity of the Curie temperature ($ T{\rm C}$ ), does not lead to any conventional single universality class expected for a standard ferromagnet; instead, the non-monotonic temperature dependence of effective critical exponents points towards more complex magnetic interactions, akin to inhomogeneous ferromagnets. Dc magnetometry complemented by higher-harmonic ac magnetic susceptibility data, further confirms the persistence of locally ordered magnetic clusters above $ T_{\rm C}$ . Theoretical analysis establishes that the enhancement of the local magnetic ordering temperature arises directly from the strengthening of exchange interactions due to the small degree of non-magnetic disorder, whereas $ T_{\rm C}$ is hardly influenced by the much larger level of magnetic-atom disorder. The combined experimental and theoretical investigations on the anti-Heusler alloy Al$ 2$ MnCu reveal and explain the unusual observation of nonmagnetic disorder-induced elevation of $ T{\rm C}$ , providing a paradigm for the counterintuitive role of disorder in magnetic materials.

Hidden Spin-Valley Hall Effect and Gate-Switchable Electric Hall Effect in 2D Altermagnets

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jiajun Lu, Mu Tian, Chaoxi Cui, Zhi-Ming Yu, Yugui Yao, Run-Wu Zhang

Hidden beneath the vanishing charge Hall response of altermagnets are spin-up and spin-down Hall currents of opposite sign—concealed, not absent—whose cancellation is enforced by symmetry. When these spin states are locked to distinct valleys, the hidden currents constitute a spin-valley Hall effect. Here we show that in 2D altermagnets with spin-valley-layer coupling (SVLC), a gate field unveils this hidden spin-valley Hall response.~By lifting the symmetry-enforced compensation between valleys with opposite layer polarizations, the field converts the hidden response into a net charge Hall signal, yielding a gate-switchable electric Hall effect (EHE) enabled by SVLC. Using collinear spin layer group (cSLG) theory, we classify all 158 altermagnetic cSLGs, identify 32 that support SVLC, and predict monolayer Mg$ _2$ Os$ _2$ (SO$ _5$ )$ _2$ and the Ca(CoN)$ _2$ -family as candidate materials. First-principles calculations for Mg$ _2$ Os$ _2$ (SO$ _5$ )$ _2$ reveal reversible valley splitting, a gate-switchable Hall response, and a Kerr rotation exceeding 1 mrad whose sign reverses together with the EHE signal. These results establish a symmetry-based route to all-electrical valley-spin control and identify altermagnets as promising platforms for high-performance valley transport and electrically switchable magneto-optics.

Indirect prediction and screening of frustrated magnetic materials using machine learning

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Regina Burganova, Irina Piyanzina, Ruslan Akhmerov, Alexander Sigov, Maxim Fedorov

Magnetic frustration is commonly quantified by the frustration index, calculated from the Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. Because both quantities are not simultaneously available for most materials, direct prediction of the magnetic frustration index remains challenging. Here, we propose a machine learning workflow for indirect prediction of the frustration index and consequent screening of potential frustrated magnetic compounds through independent prediction of Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. In our work, composition-based models implemented via the CatBoost algorithm, trained on experimental data were optimized using chemically informed dataset subdivision. The highest predictive accuracy was achieved for the rare earth-oxide model in predicting Neel and for the rare earth model in predicting Curie-Weiss. Ensemble uncertainty was incorporated through a conservative frustration index to improve the reliability of candidate selection. Screening of approximately 67000 Materials Project compounds identified 20 high-confidence frustrated magnetic candidates, including several experimentally known frustrated magnets and previously unexplored oxide systems potential for further investigation.

Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci)

Field Response of the Orbital Moment Probed by Integrated Magnetic Circular Dichroism

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Qian-Sheng Shi, Ziqiang Wang, Jian Kang, Yi Zhang

Magnetic circular dichroism (MCD) is an optical probe of orbital time reversal symmetry breaking. Although frequency integrated MCD is related to the self-rotation contribution to orbital magnetization at zero field, its magnetic response generally differs from the thermodynamic orbital susceptibility. Here, we define the MCD susceptibility and derive general expressions for multiband systems using an electromagnetic $ U(1)$ gauge invariant approach. For insulators, the response contains contributions from the non-Abelian quantum metric, Berry curvature, and field induced interband mixing. For metals, a finite temperature Green function formulation separates the smooth response into Fermi sea and Fermi surface contributions. An independent Landau level calculation confirms the expressions for the zero-field MCD moment and its linear magnetic response. In a continuum model for twisted bilayer graphene, the MCD susceptibility vanishes within the charge neutrality gap, while the thermodynamic orbital susceptibility remains markedly diamagnetic. Near the gap edges, the orbital moment term dominates the response. Features near the van Hove energies are associated with density of states enhancement. These results clarify how the two susceptibilities reflect different orbital processes even for the same electronic structure and thus establish the MCD susceptibility as a distinct probe of orbital magnetic response.

Developing the Statistical Mechanics of Evolving Assembly Spaces

New Submission | Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Anisha K. Yeddanapudi, Abhishek Sharma, Ian Seet, Leroy Cronin

A gap exists in connecting biological evolution within the framework of statistical mechanics. It has been hoped that statistical mechanics would provide us with a general predictive principle for the emergence of novelty and complexity. However, the emergence of novel functional features initiated by stochastic processes remains difficult to describe, because these processes commonly arise outside near-equilibrium or linear-response regimes. To understand how environmental influences generate diverse, open-ended, complex objects by driving physical systems to specific regions of phase space over long spatial and temporal scales, a new approach to non-equilibrium thermodynamics is necessary. Here, we present the coupling of assembly theory (AT) with statistical mechanics as a framework that works within the laws of thermodynamics, but can contextualise the processes of novelty, complexity, and evolution within these laws. We map construction processes from physical space to assembly space (AS) and introduce the concept of an assembler, a machine that constructs objects contingent on previous states and is represented by bitwise operations. Using this approach, we apply principles from stochastic thermodynamics with AT to define selection within large combinatorial spaces1 and estimate the work required to create novel and complex objects. This approach allows us to represent complex processes, such as selection, within large combinatorial spaces as a set of rules for a memory-feedback loop followed by the assembler. This framework concretely defines the relationships between thermodynamics and information theory by introducing a novel aspect of physics that uses history and causal contingency to influence the probabilistic time evolution of complex systems.

Reversible intrinsic-extrinsic crossover of the anomalous Hall effect in PrAlSi via Al/Si ratio tuning

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Huiyang Yang, Xiaohua Luo, Hongwei You, Wei Ren, Yuntao Gao, Changcai Chen, Zhishuo Zhang, Chunsheng Fang, Shengcan Ma

The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in magnetic Weyl semimetals arises from distinct microscopic origins-intrinsic Berry curvature and extrinsic scattering mechanisms-yet reversible switching between these regimes within a single material platform remains elusive. Here, we demonstrate that off-stoichiometric tuning of the Al/Si ratio (from ~1.05 to ~0.75) reversibly drives an intrinsic-extrinsic-intrinsic crossover of the AHE in PrAlSi single crystals. Across four compositions spanning a narrow Si variation (PrAl0.93Si0.89 to PrAl0.79Si1.06), the anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC) exhibits a non monotonic arch (170-1413- 507-164 {\Omega}-1cm-1). The intermediate composition PrAl0.90Si0.94 (Al/Si ~0.96) exhibits high carrier mobility, multi carrier transport, yielding a giant AHC of 1413 {\Omega}-1cm-1 dominated by extrinsic skew-scattering. The Si-poor and Si-rich compositions exhibit lower AHC values and intrinsic Berry-curvature-dominated AHE, establishing a composition-driven intrinsic-extrinsic-intrinsic crossover. Deviating from the intermediate composition suppresses mobility and reverses carrier type from hole-like to electron-like. The Al/Si ratio thus serves as a chemical knob that primarily modulates the Fermi level via compositional band filling, while off-stoichiometry-induced chemical disorder plays a subordinate role in determining the AHE mechanism. These results position off-stoichiometric PrAlSi as a tunable platform where subtle compositional variation reversibly switches the AHE mechanism.

Coexistence of Multiple Distinct Corner-State Classes via Nearest-Neighbor Couplings Alone

New Submission | Other Condensed Matter (cond-mat.other) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Luhong Su, Dongyi Wang, Shenglong Guo, Xinjiang Chen, Lei Zhou, Shaojie Ma, Mengyao Li

The coexistence of multiple classes of localized states, including different types of topological corner states, is highly desired; yet its realization typically requires sufficiently strong long-range couplings, bringing challenges to experimental realizations across a wide range of systems. Here, we demonstrate the emergence of multiple localized corner modes evolution under purely nearest-neighbor coupling conditions without long-range couplings, not even next-nearest-neighbour couplings, and experimentally confirm their coexistence in a single lattice. Our platform simultaneously hosts type-I and type-II corner states, corner localized bound states in the continuum, flat-band states, and edge states. We experimentally realize such lattice using topolectrical circuits, a system that can strictly exclude long-range coupling by design, enabling direct characterizations of both the energy-band spectrum and the spatial profiles of the localized eigenmode. This work demonstrates that solely modifying nearest-neighbor couplings can replace the role of long-range interactions in realizing multiple types of corner states, providing a deterministic and widely accessible route to multiple corner-state classes and rich localized states, with immediate potential for mode-division multiplexing and multimode topological devices.

Other Condensed Matter (cond-mat.other)

Spatial interference changes the symmetry class of a Josephson element

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Takayuki Kubo

Spatial interference can break the inversion symmetry of an effective Josephson potential even in a laterally homogeneous junction with inversion-symmetric local energy. We derive a general criterion based on harmonic-dependent complex spatial form factors: a single local harmonic remains a shifted cosine, whereas multiple harmonics can acquire phases incompatible with any common inversion center, permitting cubic interactions and three-wave mixing. Phase reflection reverses all odd-order expansion coefficients about corresponding minima while preserving the even-order ones. For a capacitively shunted junction at fixed charging energy and arbitrary offset charge, it also preserves the complete quantum spectrum, enabling reversal of the odd nonlinear sector without spectral retuning. A centered cross junction with multiple local harmonics is one concrete example. Within the imposed-field model, a three-component magnetic field can generate an inversion-asymmetric effective potential, and reversing an odd number of field components exactly reflects it without changing the quantum spectrum of the capacitively shunted junction.

Variational Minimization of Almost Strong Zero Modes in Parafermionic Wires

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Niklas Tausendpfund, Matteo Rizzi

Strongly interacting fermionic chains obeying a $ Z_n$ symmetry can host topological phases with highly degenerate ground-state manifolds. The different ground states are connected by strongly bound fractionalized edge modes called parafermionic zero modes. While exactly preserved under time evolution at zero temperature, interactions lead to a destabilization of these modes at finite energy densities, and, given a generic Hamiltonian, obtaining the lifetime of these modes is often impossible as it requires knowledge of the full spectrum of the Hamiltonian or long time evolutions. Here, we propose a novel quantum-inspired algorithm that can be used to variationally search for these almost strong zero modes in generic parafermionic chain models. We demonstrate analytically and numerically that our ansatz faithfully reproduces the exact results and finds strongly bound parafermionic operators in the non-integrable Kitaev-Hubbard chain and chiral Potts model. Our results further allow us to verify the existence of a true strong zero mode in the symmetrical limit of the chiral Potts model for $ Z_3$ -parafermions.

Approaching Irrational Quantization

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Zhihong Wu, Zhimou Chen, Siyuan Zhang, Jinyang Chen, L. N. Pfeiffer, K. W. West, Yang Liu, Xi Lin

The integer and fractional quantum Hall effects reveal that quantized physical responses can take integer and fractional values, respectively. This naturally raises the question of whether quantization can extend beyond rational numbers to an irrational value. Here we show that such a quantized response can emerge from the limiting behavior of a finite, experimentally realizable geometric network. We develop a method based on infinite geometric structures, in which a sequence of finite networks rapidly converges toward an infinite limit. Following this approach, we identify a planar network whose normalized resistance converges to the irrational value $ \sqrt{3}$ , and realize a finite approximation with high-precision geometric control. The measured normalized resistance is $ 1.735 \pm 0.001, h/e^2$ for a fully occupied Landau level in the designed pattern. Our experimental results extend the concept of quantization beyond rational values and demonstrate that an irrational quantized response can be approached through a finite physical system.

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Peter M. Piechulla, Iris Blommestein, Anne van Gerven, Henri Pelzer, Ezgi Demiröz, Jonas Englhard, Mingliang Chen, Julien Bachmann, J. Ruud van Ommen

Proton exchange membrane water electrolysis (PEMWE) is among the most promising techniques to provide hydrogen as both energy carrier and feedstock for the chemical industry, due to its compatibility with intermittent renewable electricity sources. However, a major bottleneck in scaling up PEMWE is the limited supply of the anode catalyst Ir. This work shows a novel approach to tailoring ionomer-free catalyst layers based on custom-made microporous substrates and two different atomic layer deposition (ALD) processes, both using Ir(EtCp)(CHD) precursor. Thermal ALD using O3 as counter-reactant yields mostly metallic Ir/IrOx and an electrode Ir loading of 0.21 mg/cm2. Moreover, using a low-loading ALD-made cathode, the total platinum group metal loading is only 0.24 mg/cm2. Plasma-enhanced ALD reduces the Ir loading to 0.04 mg/cm2 by tailoring the catalyst distribution, however, yields lower-activity IrO2. Finally, direct ALD catalyst deposition needs no ionomer-bearing ink. The anodes are therefore compatible with various membrane materials, as demonstrated in a CO2 electrolysis experiment with an anion exchange membrane.

Latent Heat and Raman Spectrum Change on Phase Transition of Water Including Calcium Chloride

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Dongha Kim, Changyoun Lee

This study investigates how Raman O-H stretching signatures may be able to track latent-heat-related energetics during water’s phase transitions. Prior work on latent heat and Raman analysis of phase change is reviewed to establish theoretical approach. Building on a framework that links O-H frequency shifts to hydrogen-bond strength, we relate entropy/enthalpy variations to temperature-dependent changes in peak position and integrated intensity. Emergence of Active Foams in a Minimal Cognitive Flocking Model

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Aryan Dwivedi, Diganta Bhaskar, Anil Kumar Dasanna

We investigate a minimal visual perception-driven model of self-propelled particles that interact through purely position-based cohesive steering within a finite vision cone, in the absence of velocity alignment and physical volume exclusion. Building on a previously proposed minimal cognitive model, we extend the interaction range from the previously studied short-range and distance-independent limits to an intermediate regime, enabling a systematic investigation of how a finite vision range influences collective behavior. We find that this regime exhibits a rich state diagram that combines characteristics of both limiting cases while giving rise to qualitatively new collective states. These structures arise solely from non-reciprocal vision-based interactions and are absent in both the short and long vision-range limits. Eigenstate transitions and critical transport in 3D Aubry-André model

New Submission | Disordered Systems and Neural Networks (cond-mat.dis-nn) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Pragna Das, Lev Vidmar, Miroslav Hopjan

We numerically study spectral and wave-function properties, as well as time evolution, in finite-size commensurate approximations of the 3D Aubry-André model. We explicitly show, through scaling analysis, the existence of two critical points. One of the critical points is a localization-delocalization transition in real-space, similar to the critical point in the 3D Anderson model. The other is a localization-delocalization transition in a dual momentum space, which does not have any analog in the 3D Anderson model. We estimate their values from spectral measures, such as the gap ratio and the spectral form factor, as well as from wave-function measures, such as the inverse participation ratio and the participation entropy. Additionally, we demonstrate that both critical points exhibit hallmarks of critical points between quantum ergodicity and localization. First, the eigenstates and the eigenspectrum at the critical point fulfill the mutual universal relation between the eigenspectrum compressibility $ \chi$ and the eigenstate fractal dimension $ d^{(1)}$ [S. Jiricek et al., Proc. Nat. Acad. Sci. 123, e2518027123 (2026)]. Second, the dynamics of the wave packet at the critical point is governed by the critical dynamical exponent $ z$ , characterized by the spectral fractal dimension and the dimensionality of the lattice [M. Hopjan and L. Vidmar, Phys. Rev. Lett. 135, 060401 (2025)]. Thus, similarly to the 3D Anderson model, the critical dynamical exponent is sub-diffusive with the value of $ z=3$ .

Epitaxial Oxide Thin Films with High Entropy and Anomalous Crystallinity

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Joseph Petruska, Christina M. Rost, Jon-Paul Maria, Saeed S. I. Almishal

Pulsed laser deposition (PLD) expands the compositional and structural possibilities of high-entropy oxide (HEO) thin films through far-from-equilibrium growth and kinetic arrest of metastable configurations. This review connects growth kinetics and epitaxial constraints to the roles of individual cations in shaping the resulting films. Quantum Localization in Geometric Complex Networks

New Submission | Disordered Systems and Neural Networks (cond-mat.dis-nn) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jan Lewenstein-Sanpera, Tobias Grass, M. Àngeles Serrano, Bruno Juliá-Díaz

Quantum localization, the phenomenon by which wave diffusion is halted, here understood as the superposition of propagating waves, can have many different origins, such as onsite random potentials in Anderson localization. However, some systems exhibit complex geometries and could potentially reproduce similar results without external potentials. In this work, we study how localization of single particle states is affected by the underlying topology of a simple but highly predictive complex network model: the geometric soft configuration model $ \mathbb S^1$ . By tuning the model’s temperature and heterogeneity parameters, we are able to generate different structural patterns and focus on the transition from a small-world to a non-small-world regime. Our study suggests that small-world networks tend to have extended states, which spread the whole system’s size, in contrast to non-small-world networks which tend to localize, showing how complex connectivity can play the role assigned to random energetic disorder.

Probing the wave-vector gap in nanoscale magnonics

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ondřej Wojewoda, Samer Kurdi, Toeno van der Sar, Daniel Schick, Sebastian Wintz, Alexei Maznev

Short-wavelength spin waves are central to miniaturizing magnonic devices and to studying exchange-dominated magnetization dynamics. Yet the wave-vector range between roughly 30 and 300 radians per micrometre has remained difficult to access experimentally, posing challenges for both excitation and detection. This Perspective reviews recent advances in emerging measurement techniques and discusses prospects for their future development. Optical and microwave techniques are restricted to the vicinity of the Brillouin-zone centre. Inelastic neutron, electron and resonant hard x-ray scattering cover the full Brillouin zone but lack the energy resolution needed for low-frequency magnons at intermediate wave vectors. This Perspective examines five emerging approaches that are beginning to close this gap: Mie-enhanced Brillouin light scattering, colour-centre magnetometry, magnon momentum microscopy, extreme-ultraviolet transient-grating spectroscopy, and time-resolved scanning transmission x-ray microscopy. For each, we discuss the physical principle, demonstrated capabilities, current limitations and prospects for development. Compared side by side, the techniques give complementary access to magnon frequencies, local stray fields, real-space dynamics and momentum-space distributions, and they differ in their sensitivity to coherent and incoherent excitations. We therefore discuss the main advantages and use cases of each technique and consider the practical requirements for addressing different scientific questions. We highlight opportunities for further development, including higher sensitivity, improved spatial and temporal resolution, and broader frequency and wave-vector coverage. Together, these advances could enable, for example, systematic studies of nonlinear magnon interactions at nanoscale wavelengths and investigations of emerging magnetic materials such as altermagnets.

Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall)

Spreading the Damage: How Heterogeneity Delocalizes Fracture in Polymer Networks

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Saleh Assadi, Zachary White, Radhika Joshi, Franck J. Vernerey

Fracture in polymer networks is strongly influenced by how network architecture and topology organize damage in space. Although these materials can sustain large deformations under homogeneous loading, they often fail in a brittle and flaw-sensitive manner once defects are present. Here we show that network heterogeneity is one key architectural parameter controlling this transition. Using a discrete mesoscale network model that explicitly resolves strand-level prestretch, force transmission, and irreversible bond scission, we demonstrate that heterogeneity in prestretch and strand lengths broadens crack-induced deformation, delocalizes bond rupture, and enlarges the fracture process zone. In polydisperse networks, increasing contour-length disorder broadens the prestretch distribution and systematically increases both toughness and the intrinsic length scale governing flaw sensitivity. Bimodal networks amplify this mechanism further through combined contrasts in prestretch and strand lengths, producing the largest damage zones and the highest toughness among the architectures studied. Inspired by the results, we derive a scaling estimate relating the emergent fracture-active length to the spatial range of crack-induced deformation and the logarithmic spread of strands’ remaining stretch to rupture.

Molecular-Level Evaluation of the Thermodynamic Properties of Charged Solutions from Submolar to Molar Ion Concentrations

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Sahin Buyukdagli

The accurate estimation of the ionic activity coefficients is paramount for the characterization of the electrochemical reactions driving energy storage processes as well as the biological mechanisms involving ATP hydrolysis. The numerical evaluation of the ionic activity coefficients by computational methods is rendered difficult by the infinite dilution limit involved in the integral identity between the Helmholtz free energy and the equation of state of the electrolyte. By combining a recently introduced self-consistent Debye-Huckel formalism and its analytically tractable low density limit, we develop a computationally efficient approach enabling the accurate calculation of the free energy and the related thermodynamic functions of aqueous electrolytes over a broad range of ionic concentration and electrostatic coupling strength. Extreme anharmonicity determines the superionic transition in the prototypical antifluorite Li$_2$O

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Lorenzo Bastonero, Chiara Cignarella, Nicola Marzari, Lorenzo Monacelli

Superionic conductors are promising electrolyte candidates for all-solid-state batteries, thanks to their liquid-like ionic mobility. In the prototypical lithium-ion oxide superionic conductor with antifluorite structure, the high-temperature transition to the fast-ion regime has long been attributed to volume-driven harmonic phonon softening. However, this quasi-harmonic picture neglects the strong lattice anharmonicity inherent to the fast-ion regime, leaving the microscopic origin of the transition unresolved. Here, we apply non-perturbative lattice dynamics combined with a first-principles-accurate machine-learning interatomic potential to study the phase transition in Li$ _2$ O. We demonstrate that anharmonicity, rather than volume expansion alone as commonly assumed, plays the decisive role in determining the transition temperature and the underlying mechanism. Finally, we analyze the spectral properties using the time-dependent self-consistent harmonic approximation, revealing the breakdown of the quasi-particle picture in this material. The spectral function and the infrared response provide signtures of the phase transition that can be investigated experimentally using inelastic neutron or X-ray scattering and infrared spectroscopy. All these results establish the extreme anharmonicity of Li$ _2$ O near the superionic regime and provide a lattice-dynamic framework for understanding and engineering the fast-ion transition in this type of materials.

An empirical model for deriving information on ion dynamics from $μ$SR data via the conventional Kubo-Toyabe function analysis

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ryosuke Kadono

We present an empirical model that analytically relates the parameters of the extended Kubo-Toyabe (KT) function $ G_z^{\rm EA}(t;\Delta,\nu_{\rm i},Q)$ , which describes muon spin relaxation ($ \mu$ SR) induced by jump diffusion of ions using the Edwards-Anderson parameter $ Q$ , to those of the conventional KT function $ G_z^{\rm KT}(t;\Delta_{\rm KT},\nu_{\rm KT})$ that serves as an approximation of $ G_z^{\rm EA}(t)$ . The proposed formulae successfully reproduce the anomalous temperature dependences of the linewidth $ \Delta_{\rm KT}(T)$ and the fluctuation rate $ \nu_{\rm KT}(T)$ which are artifacts observed upon analyzing $ \mu$ SR data on ion diffusion using $ G_z^{\rm KT}(t)$ . Applying this model to $ \Delta_{\rm KT}$ and $ \nu_{\rm KT}$ published in the literature of battery materials yields activation energy $ E_{\rm i}$ and pre-exponential factor $ \nu_{\rm i0}$ in the Arrhenius equation for ion diffusion, $ \nu_{\rm i}(T)=\nu_{\rm i0} e^{-E_{\rm i}/kT}$ , that are in excellent agreement with those deduced using other techniques, transforming peculiar artifacts into a powerful diagnostic tool.

Deformation-induced spectral softening drives phase separation on non-spherical membranes

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Avraham Moriel, Howard A. Stone

Models of curvature-driven membrane sorting couple composition directly to membrane bending elasticity. Here we show that shape deformations can destabilize a homogeneous state without this composition-dependent mechanical coupling. We analyze the eigenspectrum of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on deformed spheres analytically, and verify it numerically. As the deformation amplitude increases, the spectral degeneracy breaks, and the lowest nonzero eigenvalue decreases substantially. Nonrelativistic Optically-Induced Spin Torque in Noncollinear Magnets

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Zhiyuan Li, Jian Zhou

Manipulating spin using contactless and non-invasive optical approaches is one of the forefront topic in the modern spintronic science and technology. Prior studies on optically-induced spin reorientation usually scales with spin-orbit coupling strength, setting a limitation on composing heavy elements. In this work, we extend such a limit into nonrelativistic regime by exploring noncollinear magnetic configuration. Using a general spin group-theoretical investigation, we show how the symmetric operators in both spin and lattice spaces control the spin torque and quantum geometry of Bloch wavefunction–including both quantum metric and Berry curvature. We describe their site-resolved spin symmetries and unravel their selection rules. Furthermore, we adopt a Kagomé lattice model and a realistic material family to showcase the local spin torque under light irradiation. Since the nonrelativistic spin splitting usually spans on the order of electron-volt, significantly larger than typical spin-orbit induced splitting, the nonrelativistic light-induced spin torque is concomitantly much stronger. This work extends the previous opto-spintronics from relativistic to nonrelativistic limit, significantly broadening its realization platform.

Electronic Structure of a Delafossite Oxide CuAlO$_2$ in Comparison with CuCrO$_2$

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Kenta Takahashi, Ryo Kato, Mario Okawa, Tetsuji Okuda, Akira Yasui, Eiji Ikenaga, Kanta Ono, Noriaki Hamada, Tomohiko Saitoh

We investigated the electronic structure of a delafossite oxide CuAlO$ _2$ in comparison with CuCrO$ _2$ , using vacuum ultraviolet (VUV) and hard x-ray (HX) photoemission spectroscopy (PES) and band structure calculations within the local-density approximation +$ U$ scheme. Cu 3$ p! -! 3d$ resonant PES and Cu $ 2p$ core-level HX-PES give concrete evidence that the Cu valence in CuAlO$ _2$ is monovalent, which makes a sharp contrast to CuCrO$ _2$ that showed contradictory results depending upon probing methods [Yokobori \textit{et al.}, Phys. Rev. B \textbf{87}, 195124 (2013)]. We further noticed weak but detectable satellite structures due to Cu$ ^{2+}$ state in the Cu $ 2p$ HX-PES spectrum of CuCrO$ _2$ , surprisingly, without an observable main peak due to the Cu$ ^{2+}$ state. Comparisons between the valence-band HX-PES spectra and the band structure calculations revealed a significant amount of the Cu $ 4s$ and Al $ 3s$ /Cr $ 4s$ states in the valence band. The above results can reasonably be explained by the Cu $ 3d, 4s-$ Cr $ 3d$ charge transfer model that we previously proposed in the above paper, thereby demonstrating the critical role of empty $ 3d$ orbitals in the electronic structure of the copper delafossite oxides.

High-mobility spin transport mediated by indirect excitons in a van der Waals heterostructure

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Zhiwen Zhou, W. J. Brunner, Luc M. Nguyen, C. A. Lozano, G. Mercado, L. H. Fowler-Gerace, L. V. Butov

Efficient spin transport with high spin mobility is of both fundamental and practical significance for spintronics. Spin can be transferred by moving particles. However, particle scattering limits spin transfer and prevents high spin mobilities. In this work, we study the kinetics of spin transport mediated by a quantum system of indirect excitons (IXs) in a van der Waals heterostructure composed of atomically thin transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs). This spin transfer mechanism allows for the realization of efficient spin transport characterized by a high spin mobility reaching 10$ ^5$ cm$ ^2$ /(eV$ \cdot$ s), which is orders of magnitude higher than that achieved via standard diffusive spin transport in TMD materials. The suppression of IX scattering enables high-mobility spin transport.

Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall)

Vibrational Fingerprints of Competing Structural and Polar Phases in CuInP2S6

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Pegah Mohammadi, Sobhit Singh

Layered van der Waals CuInP2S6 crystals exhibit a rich sequence of temperature- and pressure-driven phase transitions, giving rise to multiple structural phases with distinct physical properties. Despite extensive experimental and theoretical efforts, unambiguous identification of these phases remains challenging because of their structural similarities and complex vibrational responses. In this work, we employ first-principles density functional theory calculations to systematically investigate the vibrational properties of the reference paraelectric C2/c and P-31c phases and their corresponding dynamically stable ferroelectric Cc and P31c phases, as well as the antiferroelectric P-1 and P-3 phases. From calculated phonon spectra and symmetry analysis, we determine the Raman- and infrared-active modes of each candidate structure and identify characteristic vibrational fingerprints that enable their phase determination through vibrational spectroscopy. Furthermore, using Porto notation, we establish polarization- and crystal-orientation-dependent Raman selection rules for different backscattering geometries and identify the modes that are expected to emerge or vanish under specific experimental configurations. Thus, our results provide a comprehensive theoretical framework for interpreting Raman and infrared measurements of CuInP2S6 and for reliably identifying its competing structural, ferroelectric, and antiferroelectric phases across temperature- and pressure-driven phase transitions.

9 pages, 4 figures

Few-Magnon Universality beyond Contact Interactions in Long-Range Spin Chains

New Submission | Quantum Gases (cond-mat.quant-gas) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Yiran Huo, Pengfei Zhang

Quantum few-body systems exhibit a rich variety of phenomena that provide fundamental insights into universality. A paradigmatic example is the Efimov effect, in which an infinite tower of three-body bound states emerges with binding energies forming a geometric sequence. Motivated by advances in trapped-ion systems, recent theoretical work has predicted that the Efimov effect can arise in certain long-range quantum spin chains, where magnons exhibit a general dispersion $ \epsilon_k=u|k|^z$ with $ z\in(0.52,0.88)$ and interact through a contact potential. Nevertheless, whether Efimov universality persists in the presence of experimentally relevant long-range interactions between magnons remains unexplored. In this work, we address this issue by investigating the two- and three-magnon problems with a model interaction potential $ V(x)=V_0/(1+|x|^\beta)$ . Our results demonstrate that few-body universality persists for any $ \beta>1$ : In the two-magnon sector, the low-energy expansion of the scattering matrix near a two-body resonance agrees with the universal form predicted by contact-interaction theory. The three-magnon bound-state spectrum exhibits Efimov scaling, with a scaling factor consistent with the theory. We further highlight the experimental relevance of our model, which can be readily realized in trapped-ion systems. Our results pave the way for quantum simulations of universal few-body physics beyond the traditional paradigm of nonrelativistic particles.

10 pages, 4 figures

Length-scale dependence of collective time reparameterization in swap dynamics

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Massimo Pica Ciamarra

Swap Monte Carlo dramatically accelerates the equilibration of deeply supercooled liquids while preserving the equilibrium Boltzmann distribution. Recent work has argued that swap and other accelerated algorithms act by reparameterizing time: different dynamics follow the same slow relaxation process, but with different clocks. Here we test this idea in a continuously polydisperse glass former. We confirm that mean collective overlaps satisfy an approximate time reparameterization, in agreement with earlier results. However, the associated clock depends systematically on the collective probe length and on the relaxation stage. Thus the observed collective collapse does not define a unique, observable-independent material time. Over the numerically accessible $ \alpha$ -relaxation regime, swap changes not only the rate of relaxation, but also its length-scale dependence. The resulting collective reparameterization is therefore only approximate and does not establish an exact dynamical equivalence between swap and reference relaxation.

Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft), Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech)

Chirally pinned flat bands, helical minibands, and bias-controlled topology of Jackiw-Rebbi states in a periodically modulated Dirac mass texture

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Tao Zhou

Flat bands amplify interactions into correlated phases, and topological bound states are robust by principle; combining the two in a model that is both exactly analyzable and experimentally accessible is therefore especially desirable. Here we show that a square-lattice model with a slowly modulated Dirac mass texture, $ m(x)=m+m_0\cos(2\pi x/L)$ with $ L$ up to hundreds of sites, provides such a setting, without engineered geometries, lattice-scale textures, or long-range hoppings. Exact nesting reduces the problem, independently at each transverse momentum $ k_y$ , to a $ 1+1$ dimensional Dirac Hamiltonian with a position-dependent mass: every simple zero of $ m(x)$ binds Jackiw-Rebbi zero modes, and the two walls of a period form an effective Su-Schrieffer-Heeger ring. The model interpolates between a chirally pinned flat band at large $ L$ , a helical miniband whose nodes are enforced by a boundary phase of the ring, and a bias-dimerized regime in which the sign of the bias sets a winding number. A central result is that the miniband bandwidth is not a single scale: a max-min measurement conflates three widths of different origin, the helical height $ W_{\rm JR}$ exponential in the period, the zone-edge upturn $ W_{\rm edge}\propto L^{-2/3}$ dominating at large $ L$ , and a bias bump exponential in $ L$ , so the flat band is robust against bias in the thermodynamic limit. We analyze two lattice realizations, a two-orbital model with modulated inter-orbital hybridization and a single-orbital bond-order texture sharing the nodal physics while leaving the van Hove points gapless, and propose experiments on cold-atom superlattices, Kekule-ordered graphene, STM designer lattices, and microwave arrays.

Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall), Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci), Quantum Gases (cond-mat.quant-gas)

Self-Avoidance Governs Re-entrant Coil-Globule Transition of Inertial Active Brownian Ring Polymers

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Rakesh Palariya, Arindam Panda, Roland G. Winkler, Sunil P Singh

Active matter systems composed of self-propelled entities exhibit a wide range of out-of-equilibrium phase behaviors and collective dynamics. Assemblies of active particles, e.g., in a linear structure, are particularly interesting, as they exhibit an intriguing interplay between activity and geometrical restrictions due to self-avoidance and entanglements. Using Langevin dynamics simulations of an active Brownian ring polymer with translational and rotational inertia, we uncover an inertia-driven re-entrant coil-globule transition in the absence of any attractive interactions. We show that rotational inertia plays a paramount role, leading, in the globular state, to spontaneous, nonzero, radially outward polarization of the active forces. Self-avoidance and entanglements break the rotational symmetry of active beads and effectively stabilize the globular states. The transition becomes increasingly sharp with increasing rotational inertia, yielding highly compact globular conformations at intermediate activities, whereas at high activity the polymer re-expands into a coil state. Scaling of the radius of gyration reveals crossovers from self-avoiding, phantom-like to compact-globular structures. Further, rotational inertia enhances transport properties, with an intermediate activity-induced Rouse-type bead diffusion and a long-time diffusion coefficient displaying a power-law dependence on the moment of inertia. Our study establishes a purely inertia-induced mechanism for controlling the collapse of active polymers, arising from spontaneous active-force polarization and self-avoidance.

Field-driven phase transition crossover in kagome antiferromagnet Mn$_{3-x}$Co$_x$Sn

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Shubhankar Paul, Chanchal Sow

Mn$ _3$ Sn exhibits a complex magnetic phase diagram with multiple symmetry-breaking phenomena. Minute electron doping at the Mn site breaks the kagome lattice symmetry in Mn$ _3$ Sn. In this work, we exploited this to tune the order of the magnetic phase transition using magnetocaloric effect in Mn$ _{3-x}$ Co$ _x$ Sn single crystals. Pristine Mn$ _3$ Sn shows metallic behaviour with a spin-reorientation transition at 278 K, which shifts to 175 K upon Co doping. Additionally, Co doping also induces a weak-ferromagnetic transition near $ \approx 210$ K. The effect of Co doping results inverse to conventional magnetocaloric effect, with enhanced magnetic entropy change. Further, the scaled entropy change reveals a field-driven magnetic phase transition crossover from first order to second order in Mn$ _{2.75}$ Co$ _{0.25}$ Sn crystal. Based on experimental data, a new phase diagram is constructed, depicting the field evolution of various magnetic orders.

Orbital cancellation and reentrant localization in a quasiperiodic trimer ladder

New Submission | Disordered Systems and Neural Networks (cond-mat.dis-nn) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Xin Wang, Pei-Jie Chang, Quanfeng Lu, Muxi Zheng, Dong Ruan, Gui-Lu long

Uniform orbital detuning can restore spatial extent even as quasiperiodic modulation strengthens. In a trimer ladder with fixed hoppings and orbital modulation signs, increasing detuning and modulation amplitude together drives successive avoided crossings that redistribute orbital weights, suppressing the projected onsite potential in both lower molecular bands while leaving the third strongly modulated. Complete-band participation scaling supports two reentrant high-participation windows, with wave-packet propagation corroborating spatial recovery at representative controls over the studied sizes and times. These diagonal cancellations leave residual interband modulation, which generates a self-energy whose magnitude depends on the molecular level separation. Gaussian quadrature improves convergence of closed-walk determinant estimates used to cross-check energy-resolved attenuation. Comparing the coupled and decoupled ladders under the same finite-size participation criterion shows that restoring the full off-diagonal modulation narrows both sampled windows, with a larger absolute contraction of the second.

Defining Thixotropy

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Yogesh M. Joshi, Ronald G. Larson

Originally coined 100 years ago to represent a shear-reversible sol-gel transition, the range of the term “thixotropy” has gradually expanded to encompass time-dependent changes in viscosity. Since this risks confusing “thixotropy” with ordinary nonlinear viscoelastic behavior, in this work we seek simple, historically faithful, quantifiable, and experimentally testable definitions of “thixotropy” that avoid this confusion. By analysing both historical tradition and published experimental data, we arrive at three complementary definitions. Definition 0 provides an idealized, purely inelastic reference useful primarily for recognizing thixotropy in a constitutive equation. Definitions 1 and 2, which are more practical, require, respectively, unusually large strains to reach steady state, and unbounded growth of the material relaxation time during rest. Definition 1 can apply in either shear-rate or stress-controlled modes, with the latter encompassing the so-called “viscosity bifurcation.” These criteria are tested across diverse materials that we identify as thixotropic based on their responses clearly exceeding their viscoelastic reference scales. The framework is deliberately phenomenological and does not require specific microscopic mechanisms, allowing diverse materials to be described within a common rheological language, as is typical for rheological terminology. We believe that these definitions provide a practical framework for assigning thixotropy a more definite place in rheological taxonomy.

Wilson-loop formulations of the spin polarization and the spin Chern number

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Kiminori Hattori, Ryuki Uchida

In this paper, we derive the spin polarization and the spin Chern number in momentum and real spaces in terms of the Wilson loop in each spin sector. For these derivations, the spin matrix in the subspace of occupied or unoccupied states is generalized by the unitary rotation to prevent the spectral gap from closing. In the presence of a finite spin gap, the spin eigenvectors recompose the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian in two spin sectors. A dimensional reduction leads to the charge polarization in each spin sector in terms of the Wilson loop along the effective Brillouin zone (BZ) in one dimension (1D). A linear combination of the charge polarizations defines the spin polarization, which naturally quantifies the spin Chern number in momentum space. It is also shown that the spin Chern number is directly derived from the local Wilson loops on a discrete 2D BZ. The theoretical treatment is further generalized for real space in a supercell framework, which can deal with ordered and disordered systems in a unified manner. In this framework, the spin Chern number in real space is formulated in terms of the Wilson loop around the BZ boundary. These formulations are used to numerically evaluate topological phases in the Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang model. Numerical results show an equivalence of the spin Chern number and the $ {\mathbb{Z}_2}$ index.

Symmetry Condition and Mechanism for the Anomalous Hall Effect in Odd-Parity Magnets

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Xun-Jiang Luo, Su-Qi Zhang, Yi-Dong Liu, Yong-Ting Shi, Ding-Fu Shao, Mingliang Tian

The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in unconventional magnets has attracted considerable attention. A giant AHE has recently been observed in the candidate $ p$ -wave magnet Gd$ _3$ (Ru$ _{1-\delta}$ Rh$ _{\delta}$ )$ _4$ Al$ _{12}$ [Nature 646, 837(2025)], while its theoretical interpretation invokes a spontaneous magnetization to break time-reversal symmetry ($ \mathcal{T}$ ), although the magnetic order itself already breaks $ \mathcal{T}$ . Consequently, the microscopic origin of the AHE in odd-parity magnets (OPMs) remains an open question. Here we establish the symmetry condition and microscopic mechanism for the AHE in OPMs. Since the OPM order is classified by the spin group whereas the AHE is governed by the magnetic group, the AHE is allowed only when the magnetic point group is ferromagnetic; conversely, it is forbidden whenever the effective time reversal $ \mathcal{T}\tau$ (with $ \tau$ a fractional translation), which is essential to the realization of the OPM, is preserved. Microscopically, OPMs generically host a hidden Zeeman field (HZF) rooted in this intrinsic $ \mathcal{T}$ breaking, and its interplay with spin-orbit coupling (SOC) generates the AHE without any external field. We demonstrate this picture with models on the square, honeycomb, and kagome lattices. In particular, a square-lattice helimagnet with sublattice-resolved SOC maps exactly onto an anisotropic Rashba–Zeeman model, whose Zeeman term is nothing but the HZF and directly yields the AHE. Our results establish the symmetry condition for the AHE in OPMs and identify the HZF–SOC interplay as a general route to realize it.

Pressure Evolution of the Superconducting Order Parameter of Kagome Superconductor CsV3Sb5

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Haiyan Zuo, Wangju Yang, Zhifan Wu, Yuqing Zhao, Qiuxia Chen, Weiming Lao, Yangpeng Qi, Hechang Lei, Haizi Cong, Cong Ren

Kagome superconductors AV3Sb5 (A = K, Rb, Cs) offer an outstanding platform for exploring the interplay among charge-density-wave (CDW) order, superconductivity (SC), and nontrivial topological electronic states. For CsV3Sb5, moderate hydrostatic pressure induces a double-dome superconducting phase diagram while monotonically suppressing the CDW phase, which signals an unconventional coupling between SC and CDW. Nevertheless, the microscopic nature of the pressure-modulated superconducting state and its relationship with CDW order remain poorly understood. Here we employ soft directional point-contact spectroscopy (SDPCS), combined with superconducting resistive measurements, to probe the superconducting order parameter of CsV3Sb5 under hydrostatic compression. Our anisotropic PCAR measurements resolve two distinct s-wave superconducting gaps along the c-axis and a strongly anisotropic gap within the ab plane. Two-band Blonder-Tinkham-Klapwijk (BTK) fits to the c-axis spectra yield a large gap satisfying 2Delta1/kBTc > 3.52 and a small gap with 2Deltas/kBTc < 3.52. The spectral-weight-averaged coupling ratio 2Deltaavg/kBTc is enhanced in the pressure window between P1 and P2, suggesting that pressure-induced modifications of the low-energy electronic structure may contribute to the evolution of superconductivity. For ab-plane junctions, the superconducting gap anisotropy is gradually suppressed above P1, accompanied by the emergence of a pronounced peak-dip-hump structure suggestive of enhanced coupling between superconducting quasiparticles and a bosonic excitation. These results shed new light on the intricate entanglement between CDW and SC in kagome superconductivity.

Universal Boolean Computation in Negatively Coupled Circle Map Lattices

New Submission | Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Priyanka D. Bhoyar, Prashant M. Gade

We investigate the computational capabilities of a one-dimensional lattice of negatively
coupled circle maps in its traveling wave regime. We interpret propagating waves as information carriers and exploit their interactions to implement Boolean logic. The logical states are encoded by the propagation direction of the traveling waves, while the presence or absence of a wave at a designated output location represents the logical output. Using controlled wave collisions, we demonstrate the realization of NAND and NOR gates along with other basic gates. Since NAND and NOR operations are functionally complete, the results establish that the traveling wave regime of the negatively coupled circle-map lattice supports universal Boolean computation in principle. The demonstrated logical operations remain valid over a finite range of the coupling strength \epsilon and map parameter \omega, indicating robustness of the computational scheme to parameter variations. Our results therefore demonstrate that computational functionality can emerge from the collective nonlinear dynamics of a coupled map lattice, providing a con- nection between the dynamical properties of traveling wave states and collision based information processing.

Dynamical-state switching controls active microrod navigation in porous media

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ana Cazorla, Maria L. Jimenez, Raul A. Rica-Alarcon, Carla Fernandez-Rico, Laura Alvarez

Adaptive search and travel strategies enable biological swimmers to explore complex environments, but remain difficult to encode in minimal synthetic microswimmers. Here, we unveil multiple dynamical and orientational states of rod-like Janus microswimmers actuated via electric fields: planar active, oriented chiral and persistent rods. We discover a hidden dynamical state after pre-orienting them, which allows us to switch on demand from strong chiral to persistent motion. We harness this phenomenological orientation and chirality switch for exploration in microstructured porous arrays. Here, strong chirality promotes local scanning but drives circular trapping and poor net displacement, whereas the pre-aligned dynamical state enables efficient obstacle sorting, and trap escape. Comparing experiments with simulations of active Brownian particles in the very same experimentally-extracted obstacle geometries, we quantify navigation efficiency via first-passage time statistics, revealing how chirality tuning optimizes the trade-off between local exploration and long-range transport.

Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft), Applied Physics (physics.app-ph)

Derjaguin-corrected Kelvin equation for finite open slits

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Alexandr Malijevský

We formulate a Derjaguin-corrected Kelvin equation for phase transitions in finite open slits. In such pores, capillary condensation is modified by the finite wall length because the condensed phase is bounded by two terminal menisci pinned at the slit ends. Starting from the macroscopic generalized Kelvin equation, we introduce a scaling approximation which replaces the implicit edge-contact-angle condition by a closed analytic expression while preserving the exact limiting behaviours. We then incorporate mesoscopic wetting-layer contributions into the finite-slit free-energy balance. The single-wall contribution naturally separates into a planar Derjaguin term and a residual finite-length correction controlled by the competition between the wall length $ H$ and the parallel correlation length $ \xi_\parallel$ . The resulting equation reduces to the standard Derjaguin-Kelvin equation for infinitely long slits and to the macroscopic finite-slit Kelvin equation when adsorbed layers are absent. Explicit forms are derived for long-ranged dispersion forces and for short-ranged forces. The theory is tested using classical density functional theory for finite slits with attractive Lennard-Jones walls and hard walls, corresponding respectively to capillary condensation and its drying analogue, capillary evaporation. The Derjaguin-corrected theory substantially improves on the purely macroscopic prediction, capturing both the correct infinite-slit limit and the observed dependence on wall length. For the short-ranged hard-wall system, the agreement with DFT remains nearly quantitative even for very narrow slits.

Phys. Rev. E 114, 045502 (2026)

Angle-Resolved Photoemission as a Sensitive Probe of Surface Quality: Sputtered versus Annealed Cu(001)

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Hyeonhee Na, Jeonghwan Oh, Jinju Lee, Hanjun Sim, Myeongun Go, Jimin Sohn, Jeonggu Kang, Byungmin Sohn

Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) requires a well-ordered surface, whose quality is routinely judged from low-energy electron diffraction (LEED) and x-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS). We compare LEED, XPS, and ARPES of a Cu(001) single crystal measured in situ at four stages of surface preparation: as-prepared, after aging for 24 h at 6 K, after Ar$ ^+$ sputtering, and after annealing. The sputtered and annealed surfaces show nearly identical $ (1\times1)$ LEED patterns and Cu 2$ p$ core levels, but the sputtered surface yields a momentum-independent ARPES spectrum, and the band dispersion recovers after annealing. We attribute this contrast to how each probe responds to sputtering-induced defects: LEED reflects the average lattice, which remains periodic, whereas ARPES reflects the electronic states, whose crystal momentum is no longer well defined. Our results indicate that a sharp LEED pattern and clean core-level spectra do not guarantee a surface suitable for ARPES.

Thermal softening of the yield stress in glasses with different stability

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Tianze Wan, Ludovic Berthier

The yielding of amorphous solids has been extensively studied in the athermal limit, but the influence of thermal fluctuations is much less understood, in particular for glasses of different stability. Using molecular dynamics simulations of a model glass where the swap Monte Carlo algorithm can be employed, we study the yield stress at finite temperature and shear rate, both in steady flow and at shear start-up, over a broad range of glass stability. Thermal fluctuations soften both the dynamic yield stress in steady state and the start-up yield stress. In all cases, the temperature dependence is compatible with the $ T^{2/3}$ law expected for thermally activated yielding near a saddle-node instability, with no systematic dependence of the exponent on stability or shear rate. Extrapolations to zero temperature recover the athermal yield stresses. The amplitude of the softening at start-up, by contrast, depends strongly on stability: highly stable glasses are much less sensitive to temperature than poorly annealed ones. A description of yielding at finite temperature must therefore account explicitly for the stability of the glass.
