Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft), Disordered Systems and Neural Networks (cond-mat.dis-nn), Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci), Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech)

14 pages, 6 figures. Submitted to J. Phys. A: Mathematical and Theoretical to its Focus Collection “Driven Amorphous Matter: Deformation, Activity, Aging and Memory”

Decoupling Trap States in Amorphous Oxide Semiconductor Transistor Using a Conductive-Semiconducting-Conductive MOS Structure

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Kai Chen, Wei Zhang, Xuanyao Fong, Shengman Li, Yunlong Li

We propose a novel amorphous oxide semiconductor (AOS)-based conductive-semiconducting-conductive (CSC) MOS test structure to effectively sense, discriminate, and analyze distinct trap-induced conductance losses in AOS transistors. In conventional test structures, non-ideal metal-AOS contacts convolute the conductance loss generated by various trap states. The CSC architecture mitigates these contact-related artifacts, enabling the decoupling of interface states and shallow states. Furthermore, the contributions from leakage, series resistance, and device-geometry effects are identified and calibrated. Our results yield bias-resolved trap profiles and reveal three distinct regimes: (1) In the depletion region, interface states dominate the conductance loss. These states exhibit sub-microsecond time constants and their density increases slightly with deeper depletion, which is associated with donor-like states. (2) From weak to moderate accumulation, shallow states become the primary contributor, displaying a Gaussian-like volumetric density-of-states distribution with response times spanning from microseconds to nanoseconds. (3) In the strong accumulation regime, interface states regain dominance with an increased state quantity, while their time constants remain invariant. These bias-resolved profiles clarify how different trap populations impact AOS transistors under distinct operating conditions, providing intuitive metrics for understanding reliability degradation. Consequently, the CSC structure offers a simple, scalable, and robust platform for trap characterization in AOS transistors.

Thermally Enhanced Pseudo-Goldstone Spin Gap in the Isotropic Helimagnet ZnCr$_2$Se$_4$

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

N. D. Andriushin, A. Sanders, J. Reuther, D. C. Peets, P. Steffens, A. Hiess, J. Lass, L. Prodan, V. Tsurkan, V. Felea, D. S. Inosov

In magnetically ordered systems, the spin gap in the magnon spectrum is normally expected to decrease on warming and close at the transition out of the long-range order. The presence of a pseudo-Goldstone gap is commonly associated with nonlinear terms in spin-wave theory. However, its precise origin and detailed properties can vary and remain under debate. Here we show by means of inelastic neutron scattering that the pseudo-Goldstone gap in the Heisenberg helimagnet ZnCr$ _2$ Se$ 4$ exhibits an atypical, nonmonotonic temperature dependence: the gap increases over much of the ordered phase before closing near $ T\mathrm{N}$ . These results align with recent spin-dynamic simulations based on an effective quartic potential in an isotropic system~[Phys. Rev. B \textbf{113}, L220411 (2026)]. As the pseudo-Goldstone spin gap in ZnCr$ _2$ Se$ _4$ is dominated by quantum effects at subkelvin temperatures, its characteristic growth on warming is caused by thermal fluctuations.

Photon echo from Maki-Thompson interference in a fluctuating s-wave superconductor

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Hui Zhou, Yuan Wan

We study theoretically the nonlinear optical response of a fluctuating s-wave superconductor driven by two successive terahertz pulses. We show that the response exhibits a photon echo signal that arises from the quantum interference between the time-reversed electron and hole trajectories in the Maki-Thompson process. By contrast, the transport of the fluctuating Cooper pairs responsible for the Aslamazov-Larkin conductivity does not generate an echo. The photon echo signal can be readily measured using terahertz two-dimensional coherent spectroscopy, highlighting its potential to distinguish different fluctuation conductivity mechanisms.

The martensitic transformations in stoichiometric Ni$ _2$ MnGa and related Heusler alloys are governed by a delicate interplay between electronic structure, magnetism, and lattice dynamics. While density functional theory (DFT) has successfully mapped the energy landscape of modulated martensites, it remains computationally prohibitive for capturing finite-temperature dynamics across relevant length and time scales. Here, we bridge this gap by constructing DFT-trained machine-learning force-fields (ML-FF) interpolated from ab initio molecular dynamics. Our results pave the way for large-scale simulations of magnetic shape memory alloys with near-DFT accuracy.

Deterministic positioning accuracy of B centers in hBN by electron irradiation

New Submission | Other Condensed Matter (cond-mat.other) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

A. Núñez Marcos, C. Arnold, J. Barjon, S. Buil, J.-P. Hermier, A. Delteil

Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is a promising material platform for quantum technologies, enabling both the realization of nanophotonic devices and the hosting of point defects that act as high-quality single-photon emitters (SPEs). Among these defects, B centers, which reproducibly emit at 436 nm, can be generated individually at predefined positions using electron beam irradiation, a crucial capability for top-down quantum photonics. Yet, the spatial accuracy of emitter creation strongly conditions the potential applications. Here, we generate arrays of individual B centers in a scanning electron microscope (SEM) with in-situ cathodoluminescence (CL) monitoring. We characterize the sources of random deviations from the target positions to identify the limitations to their deterministic positioning. In particular, we measure the distance between the electron beam and the emitter at the moment the emitter is created based on CL mapping. This approach provides a direct estimation of the spatial extent of the beam-emitter interaction. We find this contribution to be the main constraint to the emitter fabrication accuracy, yielding a standard deviation of 27 nm. Additional contributions, primarily from the finite density of UV-emitting carbon dimer precursors, increase the overall positional standard deviation to 40 nm. Our results highlight the possible improvements to the B center creation technique to reach sub-10 nm accuracy.

Molecule-intercalated van der Waals Josephson junctions uncover complex multiband superconductivity

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Daniel Margineda, David Caldevilla-Asenjo, Covadonga Álvarez-García, Daniel Tezze, Raul Arenal, Maxim Ilyn, Celia Rogero, Luis E. Hueso, Marco Gobbi

Intercalation of molecular species provides a route to tailor the electronic structure in van der Waals superconductors, yet intercalated compounds have rarely been integrated into phase-sensitive quantum devices. Here, we realize hybrid molecular Josephson junctions based on TaS2/NbSe2 heterostructures. Selective intercalation of the TaS2 electrode induces monolayer-like Ising superconductivity, while the junctions exhibit asymmetric Fraunhofer interference, field-induced and spontaneous Josephson diode effects. The zero-field diode disappears upon reversing the electrode stacking and re-emerges after intercalating the lower NbSe2 electrode. These findings indicate that the hybrid superconducting electrode hosts unconventional superconductivity underlying the nonreciprocal transport, while molecular intercalation acts as a tuning knob for its internal phase structure. This behavior is consistent with a time-reversal-symmetry-breaking multiband superconducting state characterized by a nontrivial relative phase between superconducting condensates. Our results establish hybrid molecular Josephson junctions as a quantum-device platform for phase-sensitive investigation and integration of molecularly engineered superconductors, opening opportunities to uncover and engineer unconventional superconducting phases.

Emergence of Nonlocal Hydrodynamics in Many-Body Radiative Heat Transport

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Mauricio Gomez Viloria, Florian Herz, Riccardo Messina, Philippe Ben-Abdallah

We develop a nonlocal hydrodynamic theory of radiative heat transport in many-body systems by coarse graining their microscopic electrodynamic interactions. Long-range radiative exchange naturally generates a spatially nonlocal constitutive relation in which the heat current at a given position depends on temperature gradients over an extended region, while energy conservation retains its local conservative form. Fourier transport emerges as a controlled long-wavelength limit when the interaction kernel has a finite second spatial moment. When this moment fails to converge, spatial nonlocality survives coarse graining and gives rise to non-Fourier transport. The resulting framework establishes a systematic bridge between discrete many-body fluctuational electrodynamics and a continuous description of radiative heat transport.

Scalable Quantum Monte Carlo for Shannon-Rényi Entropy: An Experiment-visitable Probe of Quantum Phases and Criticality

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Dong-Xu Liu, Zhe Wang, Zenan Liu Yi-Ming Ding, Zheng Yan

The Shannon-Rényi (SR) entropy characterizes the probability distribution of many-body wave functions in a chosen basis and is directly accessible from single-shot measurement snapshots in cold-atom experiments, yet its theoretical evaluation in interacting systems carries exponential cost. Here we construct a field-theoretic spacetime manifold for the SR entropy in which $ q$ replicas are glued at the imaginary-time boundaries, recasting it as a ratio of generalized partition functions amenable to large-scale quantum Monte Carlo simulation. On this basis we develop a reweight-annealing framework with two complementary strategies: a virtual-parameter scheme interpolating between unglued and fully glued limits via binomial-weighted partial gluing, and a real-parameter scheme using a physical coupling constant as the annealing variable within stochastic series expansion. Both reduce the computational cost from exponential to polynomial. Most importantly, we establish for the first time a finite-size scaling theory for the SR entropy near a quantum critical point, $ S_q=a(g)L^2+S_q^0\left((g-g_c)L^{1/\nu}\right)$ ; data collapse of its first derivative yields the correlation-length exponent $ \nu$ for both the O(3) and Ising universality classes, realized in the spin-$ \frac{1}{2}$ columnar dimerized Heisenberg model and the bilayer Heisenberg-Ising model. The same simulations extract the Goldstone-mode count $ N_G=2$ and the $ Z_2$ ground-state degeneracy $ \ln 2$ from subleading terms. These results elevate the SR entropy to a standalone, order-parameter-free diagnostic of quantum criticality with the same scaling status as the entanglement entropy, and close the loop between theory and quantum simulation by enabling quantitative benchmarking of experimental snapshots from state-of-the-art quantum simulators.

11 pages, 5 figures

Superconductivity from altermagnetic fluctuations: the crucial role of sublattice polarization

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Mats Barkman, Andreas Kreisel, Brian M. Andersen

Unconventional superconductivity in correlated systems often appears in the vicinity of a magnetically ordered state. This situation is also relevant for altermagnets, characterized by a nonrelativistic spin-split electronic structure in the presence of Néel order. Many proposed altermagnetic candidates, however, never cross the magnetic instability. We study superconductivity arising from altermagnetic fluctuations in such systems and show that the sublattice polarization (SLP) of the normal-state bands plays a pivotal role in determining the superconducting state. SLP, an inherent property of altermagnetic band structures that persists even without magnetic order, prevents Cooper pairs from forming between different sublattices. This suppresses pairing mediated by the nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic exchange, which favors spin-singlet superconductivity, and leaves longer-range same-sublattice bonds dominant. When the next-nearest-neighbor exchange is ferromagnetic, as expected for frustration-free Néel order, this results in spin-triplet superconductivity. This mechanism is fundamentally different from that in altermagnets with long-range Néel order, where the spin-split Fermi surfaces favor equal-spin pairing. Finally, when longer-range interactions in the disordered phase instead favor singlet pairing, SLP leads to singlet states with characteristic properties distinct from nearest-neighbor pairing states.

Optically driven ultrafast lattice contraction in a topological crystalline insulator

New Submission | Other Condensed Matter (cond-mat.other) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

M. H. Berntsen, W. Chen, D. Phuyal, J. Wu, A. Grubišić-Čabo, M. Dendzik, Q. Guo, A. Orthaber, C. Li, R. M. Geilhufe, J. Weissenrieder, O. Tjernberg

The ability to dynamically control topological order remains a central challenge in the exploration of quantum materials. Here, we investigate the lattice and electronic response of the topological crystalline insulator (TCI) Pb$ {1-x}$ Sn$ x$ Se to ultrafast optical excitation using time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (tr-ARPES) in combination with X-ray diffraction (XRD) and first-principles calculations. Static XRD measurements establish the lattice constant as the key control parameter for the topological transition, with a critical value $ a\mathrm{c}=6.073$ Åseparating the normal-insulator and topological phases. Despite generating electronic temperatures far above the topological-to-normal transition temperature $ T\mathrm{c}$ , optical excitation in the TCI phase does not induce a phase transition. Instead, it drives an ultrafast lattice contraction of $ \Delta a \approx -0.45$ pm within less than 1 ps that counteracts thermal expansion and prevents the system from reaching $ a_\mathrm{c}$ . The optical excitation therefore pushes the system deeper into the topological phase, providing a direct explanation for the absence of an optically driven transition. Total-energy calculations reveal that this behavior originates from the band inversion in the topological regime, which reverses the character of the photoinduced charge transfer and transiently strengthens atomic bonding. These findings identify band inversion as a mechanism that fundamentally alters the nonequilibrium lattice response, with general implications for ultrafast optical control of topological materials.

Theoretical Investigation of Oxygen-Vacancy Diffusion in SrTiO$_3$ using Neural Network Potentials

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Kazutaka Nishiguchi, Ryota Yamamoto, Seiichiro Ten-no

Oxygen-vacancy formation and diffusion play important roles in determining the electronic and atomic transport properties of SrTiO$ _3$ (STO). Accurate analysis of such point defects requires sufficiently large-scale and long-time molecular dynamics (MD) simulations; however, direct investigations of oxygen-vacancy dynamics using {\it ab initio} MD (AIMD) based on density functional theory (DFT) are limited to relatively small systems and short time scales because of their high computational cost. In this study, we develop a neural network potential (NNP) for pristine and oxygen-deficient STO using a reference dataset generated from AIMD simulations. The trained NNP model accurately reproduces the DFT reference energies, atomic forces, and virials for both pristine STO and STO containing an oxygen vacancy. The NNP also reproduces the supercell-size dependence of the total energies and oxygen-vacancy formation energies, including the formation energy extrapolated to the dilute limit. Large-scale and long-time MD simulations using the NNP (NNP-MD) are then performed to investigate oxygen-vacancy diffusion. The resulting oxygen-vacancy diffusion coefficients show substantially better agreement with experimental data than those obtained from conventional classical MD simulations. An Arrhenius analysis yields an oxygen-vacancy migration enthalpy of 0.64 eV, in close agreement with the experimental value of 0.67 eV. These results demonstrate that NNPs trained on first-principles data provide an accurate and computationally efficient framework for investigating defect formation and long-time vacancy transport in perovskite oxides.

Bogoliubov excitations of a Bose-Einstein condensate in the presence of the non-Hermitian skin effect

New Submission | Quantum Gases (cond-mat.quant-gas) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Carlo Panu, Ulf Peschel, Oleg A. Egorov, Elena A. Ostrovskaya, Eliezer Estrecho

The non-Hermitian skin effect is one of the most remarkable topological features of non-conservative physics. It causes a macroscopic accumulation of states at the boundaries of a confined system, opening new ways for controlling and directing waves in non-Hermitian settings. This effect has recently been observed in experiments with Bose-Einstein condensates of exciton polaritons and ultracold bosonic quantum gases. The underlying physics is captured by a mean-field Hamiltonian with a trapping potential and an imaginary gauge field. While prior work has examined the effect of the imaginary gauge field term on the condensate itself, its effect on the collective excitations on top of the condensate remained unexplored. Here, we develop a new theory for the Bogoliubov excitations in a non-Hermitian framework and systematically study their spectrum across different interaction regimes. By analysing the position expectation values, we find that while the repulsive interactions oppose the localisation of the condensate due to the skin effect, the excitations remain strongly affected by the imaginary gauge field. As a consequence, in the presence of harmonic confinement, we predict a strong shift in the dipole oscillation frequency, which can be observed in experiments. This is in contrast to Hermitian systems, where this frequency is fixed by the trap dimensions. Furthermore, we develop a consistent second-quantised field theory of the collective excitations in this non-Hermitian system. Strikingly, we find that a second-quantised treatment removes the shift of the dipole oscillation frequency entirely. This intriguing discrepancy between the two theoretical approaches to the treatment of elementary excitations in a non-Hermitian system can be resolved in experiments.

Interlayer Registry Controls Polar Topology in Twisted Bilayer h-BN

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Di Fan, Changming Ke, Shi Liu

Twisting van der Waals bilayers creates a moir’e stacking field with periodically varying local interlayer registry, enabling spatially patterned polarization. However, a systematic understanding of how this registry-controlled polar topology evolves with twist angle, temperature, and mechanical strain is still lacking. Here, using first-principles-trained deep-potential molecular dynamics simulations, we show that spatially varying interlayer registry governs the polarization texture and can be directly tuned by twist and relative layer deformation. As the twist angle decreases, the moir’e polarization texture evolves from compact, nearly circular vortex-like patterns to extended triangular AB/BA domains. This crossover arises from enhanced lattice relaxation at small twist angles, which expands low-energy, oppositely polarized AB/BA regions and confines in-plane polarization mainly to domain boundaries. Despite this reconstruction, the local in-plane winding remains tied to interlayer registry and persists up to 1000 K, indicating robust topology against thermal fluctuations. We further show that the mechanical response is dictated by whether deformation changes the relative registry: homogeneous deformation of both layers produces only weak distortions, whereas layer-selective deformation strongly reconstructs the moiré polarization texture. A mismatch-matrix registry-mapping model is developed to capture this response, explaining deformation-induced changes in vortex radii at larger twist angles and triangular-domain geometry at smaller twist angles. These results identify interlayer registry as the central control parameter for forming and tuning moir’e polar topology in twisted bilayer h-BN.

Real-space obstruction control of the shift current in two-dimensional Quantum Spin Hall insulators

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Michele Bagaglini, Mattia Iannetti, Jonas Erhardt, Jörg Schäfer, Ralph Claessen, Giorgio Sangiovanni, Gianni Profeta

The search for materials exhibiting a large bulk photovoltaic effect has recently expanded to low-band-gap systems capable of operating in the infrared regime, with potential applications in optoelectronics. Among the microscopic mechanisms contributing to this phenomenon, the shift current is one of the dominant sources of photocurrent generation. Owing to its geometric origin, the shift current is not only determined by the optical transition probability but also by the real-space displacement associated with the optical excitation, making non-centrosymmetric materials with obstructed electronic wave functions particularly promising candidates. In this work, using a tight-binding model of a quantum spin Hall insulator on a triangular lattice, we investigate the nonlinear photocurrent across trivial, quantum spin Hall, and higher-order topological insulating phases. We show that obstructed topological phases exhibit a substantially enhanced shift-current response, despite the similar electronic band structure. We identify the origin of this enhancement in optical transitions between states belonging to distinct Wannier-center sectors, which allow for a large real-space displacement upon photoexcitation and, depending on the topological phase, control the sign of the shift current. Our results extend the search for controllable bulk photovoltaic effects to materials characterized by real-space electronic obstruction and identify indenene, a monolayer of In on SiC(0001), as a promising candidate for realizing this phenomenon.

Activity-Induced Structural and Dynamical Heterogeneity in Colloidal Gels

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Santu Nath, Sudipta Mandal, Smarajit Karmakar

Active gels are intrinsically non-equilibrium materials whose structural and dynamical heterogeneity emerges from the interplay of attractive interactions, thermal fluctuations, and self-propulsion. We investigate a model active gel, simulated using large-scale Brownian dynamics simulations, to explore the effects of temperature $ T$ , active force strength $ f_0$ , persistence timescale $ \tau_p$ , and active particle concentration $ \rho_a$ . We characterize the structure of the evolving gel network through morphological analysis, while dynamical changes are quantified by the relaxation timescale and dynamical heterogeneity (DH). We show that an optimal choice of activity parameters yields a percolating gel network with faster gelation and larger voids and clusters, enhancing mechanical stability. Conversely, the structural relaxation timescale and DH decrease with increasing activity, indicating enhanced particle rearrangements and reduced spatial correlations of mobile and immobile regions. An effective temperature ($ T_\text{eff}$ ) framework for colloidal gels maps structural quantities and relaxation timescales in an active gel onto those of a passive gel. More interestingly, the growth of DH with increasing swim force can also be described by the same effective-temperature framework, in stark contrast to observations in active glasses. Our findings show that, in colloidal gels, temperature and active force compete, giving rise to similar structural ordering and, consequently, similar DH at the same $ T_\text{eff}$ . These findings highlight the crucial role of structural ordering in governing DH in active colloidal gels, as compared to active glasses.

Probing quantum nonlinear phononics in multilayer graphene with time-resolved Raman scattering

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jakob Dolgner, Michael Rübhausen, Dirk Manske

Coherently driven phonons are conventionally described classically. Nonlinear phonon-phonon interactions, however, allow an infrared drive to reshape the fluctuation spectrum and generate substantial nonthermal enhancement of phonon fluctuations, motivating a description beyond the coherent displacement alone. We benchmark classical and semiclassical approximations against the full quantum theory and find that the semiclassical treatment substantially improves agreement with the quantum dynamics. We propose nonequilibrium spontaneous Raman scattering as a sensitive probe of these effects and apply our framework to recent experiments suggesting quantum nonlinear phononics involving the $ \mathrm{E}{1u}$ and $ \mathrm{E}{2g}$ modes in multilayer graphene. At very strong pump fields, driving the $ \mathrm{E}{1u}$ mode significantly modifies the $ \mathrm{E}{2g}$ Raman spectrum; however, reproducing the reported response at experimental field strengths requires accounting for pump-induced Raman matrix element dynamics or alternative energy pathways into the $ \mathrm{E}_{2g}$ mode. Our findings extend nonlinear phononics beyond average atomic displacements to the dynamics of lattice fluctuations, highlighting the interplay between coherent motion and quantum correlations in driven materials.

Tailored VxOy Metal–Insulator-Transition Oxides for Temperature-Triggered Smart Insulation in Superconducting Coils

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Mattia Ortino (GREEN), Davide Uglietti (SPC), Andrea Testino (PSI), Nicola Casati (PSI), Alexander Steppke (PSI), Wiktoria Wnetkowska (PSI), Andrea Masi (ENEA), Kamil Sedlak (SPC)

Smart insulation is a turn-to-turn layer that insulates in normal operation and conducts only near a hot spot. It could combine the fast ramping of insulated coils with the passive energy dump of non-insulated coils. Metal-insulator transition (MIT) oxides are natural candidates, but the only one tested in a coil so far, V2O3, switches at 150-170 K, far above the 30-70 K window useful for REBCO magnets. We explore the vanadium-oxygen system to lower the transition. TGA redox processing produced multi-phase powders with an effective transition near 127 K, about 25 K below V2O3. Hydrothermal V3O7, hydrated or annealed, was far too resistive and showed no usable transition. V2O3-epoxy specimens provided a robust reference and an acceptance criterion. Cryogenic measurements revealed a reproducible thermal hysteresis, and the measured specific heat of V2O3 corrects earlier model inputs by close to two orders of magnitude. A first electro-thermal model reproduces current sharing through the smart-insulation layer. Doped V2O3 and V8O15 are identified as the next targets.

Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall)

Solubility of poorly soluble species in bulk and nanopores from grand-canonical Wang-Landau sampling

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Russell Kajouri, Adyant Agrawal, Philipp Stärk, Alexander Schlaich, Christian Holm

Poorly soluble species can have mean populations below one in small systems such as nanopores. In this regime, the grand-potential minimum, which identifies the most probable particle number, differs from the ensemble-averaged solute loading. We use grand-canonical Wang-Landau sampling (GCWLS) to calculate the density of states $ \Omega(N_\mathrm{A}, N_\mathrm{B})$ for a binary Lennard-Jones mixture of solute B in solvent A, both in bulk and in slit pores. One set of GCWLS simulations gives ensemble averages over a range of chemical potentials, whereas conventional grand-canonical Monte Carlo (GCMC) simulation requires a separate simulation at each state point. Independent GCMC simulations confirm the GCWLS ensemble averages. The solvent number distribution is nearly Gaussian, whereas the dilute solute distribution is skewed and Poisson-like. The most probable solute number therefore lies below the ensemble-averaged loading $ \langle N_\mathrm{B}\rangle$ and is zero whenever $ \langle N_\mathrm{B}\rangle<1$ . Reading the solute loading from the grand-potential minimum underestimates it and can falsely indicate that no solute is present. Defining the oversolubility as $ \mathcal{O}\mathrm{B}=x\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{conf}}/x_\mathrm{B}^{\mathrm{bulk}}$ , where $ x_\mathrm{B}$ is the solute mole fraction, we find $ \mathcal{O}\mathrm{B}\approx12$ in a pore of 3.5 particle diameters width when both wall interactions are repulsive. Adding wall-solute attraction moderately raises it to $ \approx13$ , whereas wall-solvent attraction lowers it to $ \approx3.4$ . This comparison shows that the wall-solvent interaction controls most of the enhancement. As the pore widens, $ \mathcal{O}\mathrm{B}$ decreases toward unity. In bulk and in nanopores alike, the solubility of poorly soluble species should be obtained from ensemble averages rather than from grand-potential minima.

Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft), Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech), Chemical Physics (physics.chem-ph), Computational Physics (physics.comp-ph)

Nontrivial topological properties from the cooperation of static perturbations and Floquet fields in type-II semi-Dirac semimetals

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Wen-Hao Bian, Jing Wang

In this work, we present a comprehensive theoretical study for the topological and anomalous transport properties of type-II SDSMs under three scenarios, i.e., static symmetry-breaking perturbations, periodic Floquet driving by two-frequency orthogonally polarized light (TFOPL), and their cooperative effects in the hybrid scenario. We first show that a static mass $ m_z$ opens a bulk gap and drives the system into a Chern insulator with Chern number $ C^{\rm per}=\pm 2,{\rm sgn}(m_z)$ . In comparison, TFOPL alone only shifts the positions of Dirac points and cannot open a full gap. Nevertheless, it produces a Berry curvature odd in the $ x$ -direction, yielding a finite Berry curvature dipole (BCD) along $ x$ proportional to the sine of the initial phase, while the $ y$ -component vanishes. The hybrid scenario, by contrast, exhibits a remarkably rich phenomenology. Specifically, its gap-closure criterion becomes cubic and thus admits up to three real roots when the effective parameter along the $ x$ -direction is negative, thereby producing multiple light-tunable gap-closing points and a series of topological phase transitions. In particular, the BCD exhibits a double-peak structure with strong enhancement near gap-closing manifolds and synchronized sign reversal of the full vector under external tuning. We further analyze the anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC), anomalous Nernst conductivity (ANC), and anomalous thermal Hall conductivity (ATHC). The AHC and ATHC exhibit quantized plateaus that serve as signatures of Chern-insulator phases, while the ANC, being a Fermi-surface probe, provides complementary local information and can identify light-induced gaps even when global topological invariants remain unchanged. Taken together, these results offer useful insights into the topological properties of 2D type-II SDSMs and related systems.

Topological Chiral Superconductivity from Doping Correlated Charge Transfer Insulator with Orbital Magnetism

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Wen Sun, Yuan Yang, Shou-Shu Gong, Wen Huang

Topological chiral superconductivity has been extensively studied from the perspective of electronic structures in momentum space, while its emergence from real-space processes in strongly interacting systems remains largely unexplored. Here, we study a doped charge-transfer insulator in a strongly repulsive spinless-fermion model with three sublattices and $ 2\pi$ flux per unit cell. Combining perturbative analysis, exact diagonalization, and density matrix renormalization group simulations, we establish a topological superconducting ground state with $ p+ip$ symmetry. In the large charge-transfer gap limit, chiral charge-transfer excitations generate a correlated-hopping process that provides a microscopic mechanism for electron pairing and its chiral structure. The chiral superconductivity persists into the smaller-gap regime, where chiral excitonic two-particle bound state can emerge. Our results establish a concrete real-space route to topological chiral superconductivity upon doping strongly interacting charge-transfer insulators with orbital magnetism.

Confinement-deconfinement transitions in the dimer-loop models on two-dimensional non-bipartite lattices

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Jay Pandey, Souvik Kundu, Kedar Damle

Recent work [Kundu, Damle, Phys. Rev. X 15, 011018 (2025)] has demonstrated that a class of $ S=1$ kagome lattice antiferromagnets with competing anisotropies can exhibit unusual low-temperature behavior that is best described in terms of the statistical mechanics of an interesting interpolation between the fully-packed dimer model and the fully-packed loop model on the bipartite honeycomb lattice. On this and other bipartite lattices in two and three dimension, such fully-packed dimer-loop models generically have two distinct power-law correlated Coulomb phases, both of which admit a low-energy description in terms of a fluctuating divergence-free polarization vector field [Phys. Rev. X 15, 011018 (2025); arXiv:2510.11607]. The transition between these phases corresponds to a confinement transition for the $ Z_2$ part of the corresponding $ U(1)$ flux [Phys. Rev. X 15, 011018 (2025); arXiv:2510.11607]. Here we demonstrate that a variant of such a topological confinement transition can also exist in dimer-loop models on non-bipartite lattices such as the kagome and triangular lattices although the low-energy description does not feature any conserved $ U(1)$ flux. In these non-bipartite dimer-loop models, both phases are short-range correlated liquids, and the topological distinction between them is in terms of a $ Z_2$ -valued winding number instead of the flux of a divergence-free polarization field. In spite of this key difference, this transition appears to be in the same Ising universality class as the analogous transition of the bipartite dimer-loop models. These kagome and triangular lattice dimer-loop models correspond respectively to the low-energy limit of a class of $ S=1$ antiferromagnets with competing anisotropies on the ruby lattice comprising a corner-sharing arrangement of fully-frustrated squares and a lattice of corner-sharing fully-frustrated hexagons.

Domain-wall-to-domain interconversion in a polar conducting oxide

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Martin F. Sarott, Majid Ahmadi, Johanna van Gent González, Ewout van der Veer, Mads C. Weber, Jacopo Pinna, Daniel A. Chaney, Arijit Sinha, Hamideh Hassani, Horatio R. J. Cox, Thomas Perrault, Amir Sabet Ghorabaei, Maria A. Loi, Philippe Ghosez, Bart J. Kooi, Beatriz Noheda

Domain walls in ferroic crystals are topological defects with a symmetry distinct from the domain interior, allowing them to display functionalities that are otherwise forbidden in the host material. Despite their unique properties, domain walls are strictly confined to two-dimensional interfaces, which hinders unveiling their true nature and limits their applicability. Here, we demonstrate reversible dimensionality control by expanding nanoscale domain walls into three-dimensional functional regions in polar epitaxial tungsten trioxide ($ \text{WO}_3$ ). This domain-wall-to-domain interconversion is enabled by the conducting, non-polar domain walls adopting the parent-phase structure. An alternating electric field applied through a scanning probe tip induces localized heating, which expands this domain-wall phase into the adjacent polar domains. This approach enables reversible spatial control to switch between mutually exclusive properties of domains (polarity) and domain walls (conductivity), offering a new paradigm for reconfigurable oxide electronics.

Destructive Interference of a Charge Density Wave in the Skyrmion Magnet GdRu2Si2

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

G. D. A. Wood, D. A. Mayoh, F. Orlandi, A. Bombardi, D. D. Khalyavin, P. Manuel, S. H. Moody, P. D. Hatton, R. D. Johnson, T. W. Chamberlain, O. A. Petrenko, M. R. Lees, G. Balakrishnan

In skyrmion magnets, charge density waves induce higher-order exchange interactions that can select multi-Q ground states. Using resonant X-ray scattering and neutron diffraction, we measure temperature-dependent spin-charge coupling in GdRu2Si2. A minimal Landau model with one critical exponent and two ordering temperatures reproduces all six observed order parameters. Spin-density wave components of the multi-Q ground state drive charge ordering, but the onset of a magnetic helix suppresses it via destructive interference.

Colloid-Colloid Interactions Impact Network Morphology of Confined Colloidal Drying Patterns

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Andreas Andersen Hennig, Natalia Kizilova, Ilaria Beechey-Newman, Erika Eiser

Colloidal suspensions that are confined between two closely spaced plates and left to evaporate slowly form maze-like labyrinth patterns in the final stage of evaporation. The fingers in these patterns form bifurcations and occasionally loops, which we may quantify with a skeletonization method. We compare drying patterns formed with two different salt concentrations in the droplet which changes the screening length and therefore the effective colloid interactions. We find that the junctions generally follow Murray’s law for optimal transport networks, and that the typical bifurcation angles peak around 53. Screened droplets additionally show more loopy networks, and is characterized by bands of transversal fingers before merging into a central non-fingering colloidal aggregate. Our observations suggest that colloid-colloid interactions can strongly influence the large-scale topology of the pattern while leaving the local geometry of the individual fingers unchanged.

1/f frequency-noise reduction in nanomechanical resonators by dissipation dilution

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Kostas Kanellopulos, Silvan Schmid

The frequency noise of a resonant nanomechanical sensor is well understood down to its thermomechanical limit, but the longest useful averaging time is set by a flicker, or $ 1/f$ , frequency-noise floor whose origin has remained unknown. We show that this floor is the thermal noise of the tension. By the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, the tension of a resonator with fixed ends fluctuates by an amount set by the loss angle of its material; for structural damping, these fluctuations produce $ 1/f$ frequency noise with a flicker coefficient $ h_{-1}=E k_{\mathrm{B}} T/(2\pi V\sigma_0^2 Q_{\mathrm{el}})$ , with $ Q_{\mathrm{el}}$ the intrinsic loss under elongation, that contains no free parameter and is diluted by the tensile stress $ \sigma_0$ in the same way as damping is in dissipation dilution. Silicon nitride strings and membranes from 30 to 340nm thickness follow the predicted volume scaling, the predicted $ \sigma_0^{-2}$ dilution across resonators and within stress-engineered strings, and the predicted thickness dependence, which places noise-active two-level systems in the volume and at the surfaces of the resonators, all at the absolute level set by the independently measured intrinsic loss. The same relation accounts for published data between 78 and 296K; below 78~K, the loss measured at the resonance no longer represents the loss probed by the noise, marking the limit of the structural-damping assumption. Dissipation dilution therefore lowers the $ 1/f$ floor, and the longest useful integration time of a resonant sensor is set by its prestress, volume, temperature, and intrinsic loss angle.

Emergent Spinorial Structure in Mesoscopic Irreversible Thermodynamics

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Giorgio Sonnino

We reconstruct a geometry of thermodynamic coordinates from transport coefficients within a mesoscopic canonical model. Self-adjoint flux observables determine a compatible evolution generator containing symmetric and antisymmetric transport coefficients. When the Hermitian transport tensor is positive, an exact graded first-order factorization reproduces this generator on its scalar sector. Its commutators define a spectral distance whose metric depends only on the symmetric tensor. Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can change the evolution while leaving the metric unchanged. The construction allows coordinate-dependent coefficients. In the constant Onsager regime, a Clifford representation gives the same distance and an algebra fixed by the transport matrix. Published transport data for a $ \mathrm{Bi_2Te_3}$ film give a principal-distance ratio of approximately $ 2.12$ in normalized coordinates at $ ZT\simeq0.68$ . We also examine a separate quadratic entropy-production observable and distinguish its discrete spectrum from the spectrum of the evolution generator. The spinorial representation adds no physical spin degrees of freedom.

Impact of shear and dilational membrane viscosities on capsule shape and dynamics under shear flow: a theoretical study

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Paul Regazzi, Marc Leonetti

In artificial (micro)capsules or cells such as red blood cells for example, the two-dimensional (2D) rheology of the membrane (or the shell) is viscoelastic and not only elastic. The deformation and inclination of such a capsule are theoretically studied under shear and extensional flows. The membrane mechanics is described by the Kelvin-Voigt model: the 2D dissipation with the Boussinesq-Scriven law associated to specific dilational and shear Boussinesq numbers $ Bq_d$ and $ Bq_d$ respectively while the elastic constitutive law is either Hookean, strain-softening (neoHookean) or strain-hardening (Skalak). The dynamics depends on the elastic capillary number $ Ca$ , the Poisson ratio (or $ C$ in Skalak law), the viscosity ratio $ \lambda$ , the nonlinearities included in the constitutive law and both boussinesq numbers. Three dynamics are identified in the limit of weak deformations: a tank-treading motion in the limit of small elastic capillary number with a characteristic time depending on $ \lambda$ , $ Bq_s$ and $ Bq_d$ , an oscillation without any elasticity and a damped oscillation leading to a tank-treading motion in the limit of large Boussinesq numbers. In all cases, both shear and dilational 2D dissipations contribute differently leading to non-trivial results. In the case of a Skalak law with $ C=1$ , $ Bq=Bq_s=Bq_d$ and $ Bq>>\lambda$ , the deformations in the limits $ Ca<<1$ , $ Bq=O(1)$ and $ Ca=O(1)$ , $ Bq>>1$ determined in the seminal work of \cite{barthes1985role} are recovered.

Transport Determines Spectral Distance in Mesoscopic Thermodynamics

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Giorgio Sonnino

We derive a distance between thermodynamic states from a positive Hermitian transport tensor within a mesoscopic canonical model. An exact first-order factorization of the evolution generator gives a commutator norm and the metric $ G=2g^{-1}$ , where $ g$ denotes the symmetric transport tensor. Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can change the evolution, but they leave this distance unchanged. The construction allows coordinate-dependent coefficients. For constant Onsager coefficients, a Clifford representation gives the same metric. In a two-channel thermoelectric system, the ratio of normalized principal distances is $ \sqrt{1+ZT}+\sqrt{ZT}$ . Transport coupling thus fixes the anisotropy of this metric. The construction requires neither an independent metric nor a physical spin variable.

Engineering Ferroelectric Phases in BaTiO$_3$ Films via Buffer-Layer Induced Misfit Strain Reversal

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

A.S. Mikheykin (1), N.V. Lyanguzov (1), A.S. Konstantinov (1), V.B. Shirokov (1 and 2), E.D. Linnik (1), T. Bondar (1), A.G. Razumnaya (3) ((1) Southern Federal University, (2) Federal Research Centre The Southern Scientific Centre of the Russian Academy of Sciences (3) Jožef Stefan Institute)

Controlling ferroelectric phase symmetry through strain engineering is crucial for tailoring polarization topology and functional properties in oxide-based devices. Here, we show that a thin conductive buffer layer can override substrate-induced strain in BaTiO$ _3$ (BTO) films on MgO. Although the lattice parameter of MgO(001) would be expected to induce tensile misfit strain, in 500~nm BaTiO$ _3$ films the thermal-expansion mismatch dominates, resulting in effective compressive stress upon cooling. Inserting a 50-nm La$ _{0.67}$ Sr$ _{0.33}$ MnO$ _3$ (LSMO) buffer layer reverses the effective strain state from compressive to tensile. Using temperature-dependent polarized Raman spectroscopy, X-ray diffraction, and piezoresponse force microscopy, we reveal an unconventional phase sequence upon cooling: paraelectric $ \rightarrow$ orthorhombic ($ \textit{aa}$ -phase, $ \sim$ 407 K) $ \rightarrow$ monoclinic ($ \textit{r}$ -phase, $ \sim$ 300-345 K), characterized in-plane and tilted polarization components. The LSMO layer not only serves as a bottom electrode but also modifies the strain state, while interfacial charge redistribution induces a built-in electric field, evidenced by self-polarization and asymmetric PFM hysteresis. These findings highlight the critical role of conductive buffer layers in controlling ferroelectric symmetry and enabling oxide heterostructures with tailored polarization textures for low-power spintronics, tunable microwave devices, and neuromorphic computing.

Quantum Ising transition on the Cayley tree: boundary criticality, universality, and an effective Bethe line

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Pietro Richelli, Natalia Chepiga

We investigate quantum criticality in the transverse-field Ising model on finite Cayley trees and demonstrate that their extensive boundary fundamentally changes the nature of the quantum phase transition compared with the infinite, boundary-free Bethe lattice. Using tree tensor-network simulations for systems with up to $ N=12286$ sites, we identify a boundary-driven continuous transition at $ h_x^c\approx1.76$ , distinct from the mean-field Bethe-lattice transition at $ h_x^c\approx2.23$ . We characterize this transition through finite-size scaling of the magnetization and magnetic susceptibility and extract a consistent set of critical exponents. By introducing next-nearest-neighbor interactions, we demonstrate the robustness of the extracted critical exponents, providing numerical argument for the Ising boundary universality class on the Cayley tree. Furthermore, we show that the physics of the boundary-free Bethe lattice can be efficiently recovered on a finite Cayley tree by fine-tuning a local boundary field. The resulting \textit{effective Bethe line}, whose existence is tied to the mean-field nature of the bulk transition, effectively screens the extensive boundary and restores both the critical point and the mean-field critical scaling of the infinite Bethe lattice.

Information Entropy as a Sensitive Diagnostic of Relaxation Dynamics in a Quasiperiodic Mott Insulator

New Submission | Quantum Gases (cond-mat.quant-gas) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

N. D. Chavda, Antônio M. S. Macêdo, Robin P Sagar, Barnali Chakrabarti

We study the nonequilibrium dynamics of a bosonic Mott insulator following a sudden quench from a periodic to a quasiperiodic lattice. Using ab initio multiconfigurational (MCTDHB) continuum many-body dynamics, we characterize the evolution through momentum distributions, fragmenta- tion, correlation functions, and information entropy. We find that melting of Mott correlations does not necessarily imply relaxation of the many-body state. In the weakly interacting regime, the initial Mott-like correlation undergo recurrent melting and partial recovery, while the information entropy displays large, intermittent fluctuations within the simulated time window. In contrast, a strongly interacting and fully fragmented Mott insulator retains its characteristic Mott correla- tions, up to partial distortions, while the many-body information entropy approaches a well-defined stationary regime plateau with comparatively small fluctuations. These results indicate that Mott- correlation dynamics and many-body relaxation can be dissociated in this finite system and support the use of information entropy, evaluated in the multiconfigurational representation, as a sensitive complementary diagnostic of nonequilibrium relaxation.

Self-phoresis on a one-dimensional periodic domain

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Leah Anderson, David S. Dean

We study the spontaneous motion and collective dynamics of self-phoretic particles on a one-dimensional periodic domain. The particles act as localized chemical sources (of a particle-dependent strength) of a field and the particles themselves respond to the gradients of the field they generate (with a particle-dependent phoretic mobility). The chemical field itself diffuses and evaporates. For a single particle, we derive the exact threshold for spontaneous propulsion and establish the emergence of stable constant-velocity states near onset through a supercritical pitchfork bifurcation. We then consider an active source coupled to a non-emitting tracer and determine the conditions for slipping and stable phase-locked motion. For two mutually coupled sources, the stationary antipodal configuration can lose stability through either a pitchfork or a Hopf bifurcation, depending on the relative source strengths and phoretic mobilities. We determine the competing instability thresholds and compare them with direct numerical simulations of the coupled particle-field equations. Beyond onset, the resulting translating and oscillatory states provide qualitative counterparts to modes of motion observed experimentally for camphor disks in annular water channels. These results connect the onset of individual propulsion, tracer entrainment, and collective motion within a common analytically tractable framework.

Molecular Precursor Chemistry Shapes Early-Stage Reaction Pathways in Nitrogen-Doped Carbon Nanotube Synthesis

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Narayan N. Som, Lukasz Nowicki, Tomasz Gizewski, Karolina Z. Milowska, Anna Wroblewska, Caghan Tuncer, Maciej Trzaskowski, Olga Iwasinska-Kowalska, Tomasz Plocinski, Mariusz Zdrojek, Krzysztof K. Koziol, Malgorzata Jakubowska, Agnieszka Lekawa-Raus

The structural characteristics of carbon nanotubes are usually discussed in terms of processes occurring during nanotube nucleation and subsequent growth from an already formed catalyst nanoparticle. Here, we show that their structural trajectory can begin much earlier, within the first nanoseconds of precursor decomposition and catalyst formation. We combine experiment and reactive molecular dynamics to compare nitrogen-free CNT synthesis with two closely related aromatic nitrogen precursors, pyridine and pyrazine, containing one and two nitrogen atoms per molecule, respectively. Experimentally, both nitrogen-containing precursors promote bamboo-like CNT formation and produce substantially more structurally disordered CNTs than the nitrogen-free reference. Notably, nitrogen incorporation does not scale simply with the amount of nitrogen supplied, demonstrating a strong contribution from precursor chemistry. Reactive molecular dynamics reveals that nitrogen-containing precursors significantly modify local Fe-carbon and Fe-SiO2 interactions during catalyst evolution, while pyridine and pyrazine exhibit distinct decomposition pathways, with pyrazine showing significantly greater decomposition within the simulated timescale. These results demonstrate that precursor chemistry influences the local chemical environment and early catalyst evolution, highlighting its potential role in the initial stages of CNT growth.

Trust-Limited Machine-Learning-Accelerated Nudged Elastic Band Calculations for Small-Polaron Hopping in Rutile TiO2

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ashish Kumar Singh, Irina V. Lebedeva

Nudged elastic band (NEB) calculations of small-polaron hopping are demanding because nuclear motion is coupled to a self-consistent localized electronic state. Near the charge-transfer region, slow self-consistent-field convergence, localization switching, and competing electronic solutions complicate surrogate-assisted optimization. We develop a Gaussian-process-assisted NEB workflow for this regime and demonstrate electron small-polaron hopping in rutile TiO2 using spin-polarized density functional theory with a Hubbard U correction (DFT+U ) with SIESTA. A joint energy-force Gaussian process (GP) with a Matérn covariance model advances the elastic band, while active-atom root-mean-square and maximum-atom displacement limits constrain surrogate motion relative to evaluated configurations. Reaching these limits triggers first-principles sampling without requiring full surrogate convergence. Three independently DFT-verified 96-atom [001] calculations using 11, 13, and 15 images and different surrogate settings and GP parameterizations yield barriers of 0.1645, 0.1637, and 0.1640 eV, respectively, spanning 0.793 meV. In the 15-image calculation, repeated central-image sampling activates a separately calibrated local GP during surrogate optimization. Final full-band DFT verification gives a maximum projected NEB force of 0.0346 eV/Å. A 324-atom calculation yields a lower barrier of 0.1302 eV, indicating supercell sensitivity and demonstrating feasibility at larger GP observation dimension. The workflow combines controlled surrogate extrapolation, targeted charge-transfer-region interpolation, and independent DFT verification to obtain polaron migration pathways, emphasizing reproducible barriers and full-band force convergence.

Magnetic properties and excitation spectrum of the S = 1/2 three-legged spin ladder, LiMoOP2O7

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Aly H Abdeldaim, Alexander A. Tsirlin, Jacques Ollivier, Clemens Ritter, Robin S. Perry, Lucy Clark, Gøran J. Nilsen

We report a comprehensive experimental study including density functional theory calculations detailing the magnetic ground state of the $ S = 1/2$ Mo$ ^{5+}$ pyrophosphate, LiMoOP2O7, and its magnetic excitations. Neutron powder diffraction measurements confirm the $ P2_1/n$ structural model which is comprised of a three-legged ladder of distorted Mo$ ^{5+}$ octahedra. Magnetometry and specific heat reveal a long-range magnetic ordering transition at $ T_\textrm{N} = 3.5$ ~K and a field-induced transition at $ \mu_{0}$ H$ _\textrm{c}$ = 1 T. Mutual linearity from classical response theory

New Submission | Statistical Mechanics (cond-mat.stat-mech) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Timur Aslyamov, Massimiliano Esposito

We unify known mutual-linearity relations for Markov jump processes and chemical reaction networks within a common framework based on classical finite-response theory. In a Markov jump process, an exact finite-response identity shows that all stationary probability changes share a common factor when the rates of one edge change. Eliminating this factor gives the relations between probabilities, observables, and currents, even far from equilibrium. We also explain how the same argument applies when one coefficient of a linear system changes and recovers the known relations in Laplace space and between stationary complex activities in chemical reaction networks.

Mutual information across the commensurate–incommensurate crossover and near the Kosterlitz–Thouless point of the spin-$\tfrac12$ $J_1$–$J_2$ Heisenberg chain

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Wuttichai Pankeaw, Pruet Kalasuwan

What quantum mutual information adds to conventional correlation functions is often unclear. We study the spin-1/2 $ J_1$ -$ J_2$ Heisenberg chain, using DMRG on infinite chains and periodic rings and an SU(2) decomposition of the mutual information into correlation channels. For singlet ground states the two-site mutual information is an exact function of the spin correlator, so any additional content must come from blocks. In the dimerized phase, blocks resolve bond-energy correlations in the SU(2)-scalar channel without an independent asymptotic length: singlet and triplet correlation lengths agree to about 1%. The scalar channel dominates block mutual information at large distances in the commensurate regime up to $ J_2/J_1=0.40$ , but falls to a few percent beyond the disorder point, which coincides numerically with the Majumdar-Ghosh point $ J_2/J_1=1/2$ ; the structure-factor peak splits only at the Lifshitz point $ J_2/J_1=0.520622(2)$ . On the gapless side we compare block mutual information on rings extrapolated to infinite size with the two-interval prediction of SU(2)$ _1$ conformal field theory across the Kosterlitz-Thouless point $ J_2/J_1=0.241167$ . There, for block lengths $ l\le6$ , the correction decays as $ l^{-p}$ with $ p=1.96$ -$ 2.07$ , and any logarithmic term is at least 60 times smaller than in the Heisenberg chain. Expanding in the marginal coupling (from level spectroscopy) the first-order coefficient is consistent with $ l^{-2}$ scaling and the second-order coefficient is positive for $ x\le1/4$ , suggesting that the mutual information relative to the field-theory value becomes stationary at the KT point for large blocks. The scalar-channel share approaches 1/4 at small cross ratio $ x$ , as expected from SO(4) symmetry; under two stated assumptions its first-order response to the coupling is $ (3/8)\ln x-5/8$ at small $ x$ , matched by the $ l=4$ data within 1-3% at $ x\le1/9$ .

Microscopic Modeling of Interlayer Tunneling in Twisted Bilayer Cuprates

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Siddhant Panda, Andreas Kreisel, Laura Fanfarillo, P. J. Hirschfeld

The field of twistronics, recently extended to twisted bilayer cuprate systems, has generated considerable interest due to the prediction of $ d+id’$ states close to a $ 45^\circ$ twist, as suggested by multiple experimental probes. Theoretical studies of these systems using a variety of models and many-body theoretical techniques have revealed multiple competing order parameters. The large variability in experimental results and theoretical predictions demands an accurate microscopic model for these systems, in particular a more realistic description of the interlayer tunneling. In this study, we fit the in-plane hopping and compute the interlayer tunneling amplitudes by calculating the orbital overlap between $ d_{x^2-y^2}$ wave functions at each site of the twisted square-lattice model. We observe sign-changing tunneling amplitudes, a strong twist-angle dependence of the direct tunneling term, and long-range tunneling amplitudes that often dominate over direct tunneling. We investigate the band structure, Fermi surfaces, and spectral functions in comparison with those obtained from the phenomenological model and find a substantial redistribution of spectral weight that modifies the competition between different superconducting ground states. We determine the phase diagram as a function of twist angle, filling, and direct tunneling strength, which is controlled by the $ c$ -axis distance between the monolayers, and observe a reduction in the region of time-reversal-symmetry-breaking (TRSB) $ d+id’$ states compared with the phenomenological model. Finally, we investigate the Josephson critical current across the phase diagram and find strongly suppressed critical currents close to a $ 45^\circ$ twist, consistent with experimental observations.

First-fracture kinetics in periodic chains: from free-energy landscapes and dynamical recrossing to absolute rates

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Aristotelis P. Sgouros

The nearly collinear configuration of highly stretched polymer chains before fracture motivates one-dimensional models. At fixed extension, stretching one bond redistributes length among the others, coupling rupture to the whole chain. In this work, I develop a framework for fracture-healing rates in periodic Morse chains, combining deterministic theories, equilibrium simulations and a quasi-deterministic approach using equilibrium fluxes and barrier-top trajectories. Constrained-partition calculations closely reproduce simulated populations and intact-state tensions. An intact-reservoir potential of mean force (PMF) supplies the first-fracture surface probability, while recrossing corrects the attempt flux without a rate-fitted prefactor. Independent rate tests identify an operational chain-length boundary for accurate two-state kinetics; failed-return durations decrease relative to population relaxation as chains grow. Kramers-Grote-Hynes (KGH), nonlinear reactive-flux and equilibrium rates show excellent agreement in this regime. KGH predicts rates away from coexistence and a chain-dependent local activation length dominated by the barrier response. Rates decrease approximately exponentially before declining more gently for longer chains: barrier growth dominates, additional fracture sites partly compensate, and transmission varies more weakly. Beyond coexistence, long chains can fracture practically irreversibly because healing becomes too rare to observe. Projected committor crossings, where the configuration-averaged probability of reaching the fractured core first is one half, lie near site-PMF maxima, although configurations at equal bond length retain different fracture probabilities. Multiple fractures remain negligible near coexistence through at least $ N=50$ . The framework could supply state-dependent rates to mesoscopic fracture models of polymer networks.

First-Principles Calculations of the Electron-Hole-Pair Creation Energy in Semiconductors and Dielectrics

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Dallin O. Nielsen, Massimo V. Fischetti

We present first-principles calculations of the electron-hole-pair creation energy in semiconductors and dielectrics, focusing in particular on AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2. We have employed density functional theory (DFT) to calculate the band structure, density-functional perturbation theory to obtain the phonon dispersion, and time-dependent DFT (TDDFT) to calculate the electron energy loss function (via the dielectric function). We performed full-band Monte Carlo simulations of electron thermalization for energies up to ~100 eV and calculated the average energy required to excite an electron-hole pair. We calculated pair-creation energies of approximately 12.2, 6.5, 7.3, 3.10, and 16.7 eV/pair for AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2, respectively. These values are in good agreement with measured data, with the largest discrepancy being that of Si (about 14.9% lower than the accepted experimental value of ~3.6 eV/pair). To improve the agreement for Si, we used the GW approach and the Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE) to calculate the quasiparticle shifts of the electronic band structure and the dielectric function, respectively. Finally, using the GW/BSE data, we calculated a creation energy of ~3.50 eV/pair. We find that the marked improvement in the accuracy of the calculated creation energy, when using the GW/BSE approach, is primarily attributable to the slight decrease in the magnitude of the electron energy-loss function when using the GW/BSE approach for energies below ~10-15 eV. Overall, it is clear that the competition between phonon emission and ionization near the intersection of the phonon scattering and ionization rates is largely responsible for determining the value of the creation energy, with the other major factor being the location and shape of the plasmon peak in the energy-loss function.

Emergent low-energy thermodynamics and universal $B/T$ scaling in $α$-RuCl$_3$

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

A. Khansili, B. J. Ramshaw, A. Bangura, H.-Y. Kee, R. D. McDonald, F. Assaad, A. Rydh, A. Shekhter, K. A. Modic

Quantum spin liquids are exotic states of matter in which strongly interacting spins remain disordered down to zero temperature and host fractionalized excitations. This has motivated an extensive search for their realization in materials. Among candidate systems, $ \alpha$ -RuCl$ _3$ has drawn particular attention as a platform for the quantum spin liquid proposed by Kitaev, in which the low-energy excitations in applied magnetic fields are interpreted as itinerant Majorana fermions and local flux excitations. However, conflicting experimental signatures have cast doubt on whether a Kitaev quantum spin liquid-like state is really the right starting point to interpret the low-energy states in $ \alpha$ -RuCl$ _3$ , motivating a closer look at the nature of these excitations. Here we report an extensive high field study of specific heat up to 35 T on $ \alpha$ -RuCl$ _3$ . We find that the low-energy thermodynamics exhibit universal $ B/T$ scaling with a single effective energy scale proportional to the applied field as long as the magnetic field is not close to the $ c$ -axis. This behavior persists continuously from the antiferromagnetic phase boundary to the highest fields, suggesting that the perturbative Kitaev limit, with its Majorana and flux excitations, may not be an appropriate starting point for describing the low-energy thermodynamics of $ \alpha$ -RuCl$ _3$ for fields away from the $ c$ -axis.

High-Efficiency Field-Free Superconducting Diodes in Mo$_2$N/FePt Bilayers

New Submission | Superconductivity (cond-mat.supr-con) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Juan S. Cruz, Emilio De Biasi, Juan C. Zapata, Yeonkyu Lee, Jeehoon Kim, Nestor Haberkorn

Near-ideal, field-free superconducting diode behavior is demonstrated in room-temperature-grown Mo$ _2$ N/FePt bilayers. The response is consistent with remanent FePt stripe domains producing polarity-dependent thresholds for flux entry and vortex motion. Micromagnetic simulations reveal a periodic stray-field modulation across the stripes and asymmetric field enhancement near their ends at the bridge edges. One current polarity remains nearly dissipationless up to a sizable fraction of the estimated depairing current, whereas the opposite polarity develops dissipation at much lower current, yielding diode efficiencies up to approximately 85% in the thinner bilayer. The nonreciprocity can be continuously tuned by the in-plane magnetic preparation angle and reversibly switched by reverse-field excursions through the hysteretic reversal of the FePt layer. These results support edge-controlled flux entry and vortex motion as a route to high-efficiency superconducting diodes.

Microscopic Theory of the Acoustic Faraday Effect in Spin-Orbit Coupled Magnets

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Antoine Matar, Haoting Xu, Hae-Young Kee

The acoustic Faraday effect probes phonon Hall viscosity through the rotation of transverse acoustic polarization, conventionally interpreted in terms of circular birefringence. We show that magnetic order breaking rotational symmetry generates anisotropic phonon responses and elliptically polarized eigenmodes, so that Faraday rotation cannot be attributed to Hall viscosity alone. To elucidate their microscopic origin and consequences, we derive spin-phonon couplings from a generic $ J$ -$ K$ -$ \Gamma$ -$ \Gamma’$ model with interlayer coupling, identifying transverse strain couplings for propagation along the $ c$ -axis arising from the interplay of Kitaev $ (K)$ and $ \Gamma$ interactions, along with strain-induced $ g$ -tensor contributions. Incorporating these couplings into our theory for the Kitaev spin-liquid candidate $ \alpha$ -RuCl$ _3$ , we find that symmetry breaking modifies the Faraday response, revealing a Hall viscosity approximately twice the value inferred assuming rotational symmetry. Our results establish a microscopic framework beyond circular birefringence, refining the connection between acoustic Faraday rotation and phonon Hall viscosity in symmetry-broken spin-orbit-coupled magnets.

Honeycomb Crystal in a Mass-Asymmetric Electron-Hole Bilayer

New Submission | Strongly Correlated Electrons (cond-mat.str-el) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Shengdu Chai, Wangqian Miao, Yuqiang Li, Lei Wang, Xi Dai, X.C. Xie

A two-dimensional electron gas in a uniform neutralizing background forms a triangular Wigner crystal at low density. In a strongly mass-asymmetric electron-hole bilayer, localized heavy holes instead provide a nonuniform positive background that supports an electron crystal at densities two orders of magnitude higher than for a homogeneous electron gas in the same material. Here we show that correlated mobile electrons can overturn the preference for triangular order, stabilizing a honeycomb electron-hole crystal. Using the powerful real-space neural-network variational Monte Carlo in the Born-Oppenheimer limit with fixed holes, we find that increasing the density drives a transition from a triangular electron-hole crystal to a honeycomb crystal, which eventually melts into itinerant electrons coexisting with the honeycomb or the triangular hole lattice at fixed interlayer separation. Relative to triangular order, honeycomb order emerges when gains in electronic kinetic energy and electron-hole attraction outweigh its higher intralayer Coulomb repulsion, a threshold never reached in the rigid point-dipole model. Moreover, the honeycomb hole lattice supports paramagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and stripe-ordered spin states. Complex polarization indicates a metal-insulator transition, while the states on the honeycomb hole lattice are consistent with a stripe-ordered Dirac semimetal and paramagnetic or antiferromagnetic Mott insulators via mapping to the half-filled Hubbard model. We further propose a BLG/hBN/WS$ _2$ -WSe$ _2$ heterostructure as a platform for the predicted honeycomb crystal, at an experimentally feasible density $ n\sim10^{11}~\text{cm}^{-2}$ .

Bifurcation Structure of Double Hysteresis and Isolated Branches in a Parametric Duffing Nanoresonator

New Submission | Mesoscale and Nanoscale Physics (cond-mat.mes-hall) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Dingda Zhang, Maria Kallergi, Chanyoung So, Eva M. Weig, Ahmed A. Barakat

Direct and parametric driving together generate nonlinear response structures beyond conventional Duffing hysteresis, including multiple switching regions and isolated branches. Understanding how these structures are connected requires identifying the bifurcations that reorganize the response as the parametric excitation is varied. At a phase difference of 45 degrees between the direct and parametric excitations, we experimentally and analytically determine how the steady-state response changes as the parametric excitation is increased. A single resonance peak first splits into two while the response remains single valued. Two successive cusp bifurcations then create two saddle-node pairs and establish a double-hysteretic response. At the parametric-instability threshold, one saddle node of each pair is displaced towards unbounded amplitude and detuning, and an isolated-branch structure emerges above threshold. A parametric representation of the steady state distinguishes amplitude extrema from genuine saddle-node bifurcations and provides analytical conditions for these changes. Measurements on a dielectrically actuated pre-stressed SiN nanostring reproduce the predicted response and associated switching behavior. These results establish the bifurcation pathway connecting the different response structures at this phase configuration, providing a systematic basis for controlling multistability and switching in parametrically driven nonlinear resonators.

Capturing Coordination Core Topology in Metal–Organic Frameworks Using Persistent Mayer Homology

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Caleb Simiyu Khaemba, Chun-Long Chen, Guo-Wei Wei

Local coordination cores are critically important in metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) and polyoxometalates (POMs), as they govern the fundamental chemical and structural properties of these materials. Existing methods, including element-resolved persistent homology (ERPH), have limited ability to translate local coordination cores into quantitative, scalable, and interpretable information. Here, we introduce persistent Mayer homology (PMH) to enable the detection of the geometric variability invisible by classical topology. By focusing on coordination cores formed by periodic metal-centered heavy-atom, carbon, and donor channels, PMH distinguishes different arrangements of the local coordination environment, linker framework, and potential coordinating atoms. We examine explainable PMH of orders $ N=3$ and $ N=5$ and separately combine each with ERPH, which characterizes the multiscale organization of chemically defined element groups across the extended framework, to predict eight nitrogen- and oxygen-related adsorption and self-diffusion properties. The resulting coordination-core PMH representations consistently improve predictive performance over ERPH alone, with the third-order Mayer representation providing the strongest overall performance. These results demonstrate that the inner coordination configurations captured by PMH contain essential structural information that is not fully represented by conventional element-resolved topological descriptors. This finding provides a quantitative interpretable topological framework for understanding local coordination environments and may facilitate the rational design of MOFs and POM-based materials.

Symmetry-constrained coexistence of charge-three and charge-one Weyl points in nonmagnetic crystals

New Submission | Materials Science (cond-mat.mtrl-sci) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

Ze-Xin Xue, Ke-Xin Pang, Yun-Yun Bai, Yanfeng Ge, Yan Gao

High-charge Weyl semimetals (WSMs), particularly those hosting charge-three Weyl point (C-3 WPs), have attracted considerable interest owing to their unconventional topological properties and enhanced boundary phenomena. However, ideal C-3 WSMs remain rare, and the symmetry-constrained coexistence of charge-three and charge-one Weyl point (C-1 WPs) in nonmagnetic crystals remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate a representative eight-node Weyl configuration, consisting of two C-3 WPs and six C-1 WPs with opposite chirality, corresponding to two identical $ {3,-1,-1,-1}$ irreducible Weyl molecules. By combining topological charge conservation with space-group symmetry analysis, we systematically classify all 230 space groups and identify 12 and 23 candidate groups without and with spin–orbit coupling (SOC), respectively. First-principles calculations without SOC reveal that the boron allotropes TBR-B$ _{48}$ and chiral OBD-B$ _{48}$ realize this configuration, hosting hourglass-type and non-hourglass-type C-3 WPs, respectively. Surface-state calculations reveal six Fermi-arc branches at the shared projection of the charge-three pair and three at each individual projection. Reversing the structural chirality of OBD-B$ _{48}$ reverses the signs of all Weyl charges, establishing a direct correspondence between crystal and momentum-space chirality. Furthermore, breaking the protecting $ C_6$ symmetry in TBR-B$ _{48}$ splits each C-3 WP into three C-1 WPs and reconstructs the surface Fermi arcs. Our work establishes symmetry-based guidelines for designing and searching for nonmagnetic WSMs hosting charge-three and charge-one Weyl-point coexistence.

Electrical control of orbital magnetism is a central goal of orbitronics. While current-induced orbital polarization has recently been demonstrated in inversion-asymmetric systems, nonvolatile electrical control of an equilibrium orbital moment has remained elusive.

José Solano Córdova, Jone Mencos, Richard Schlitz, Quentin Rossi, Denise Reustlen, Javed Ali, Sebastian Sailler, Michaela Lammel, Matthieu Bailleul, Daniel Stoeffler, Felix Casanova, Sebastian T. B. Goennenwein, Helena Reichlova

Electrical excitation and detection of propagating spin waves are key elements of magnonic devices. Skyrmion gears: translation-to-rotation conversion of skyrmion clusters along periodic substrates

New Submission | Soft Condensed Matter (cond-mat.soft) | 2026-10-14 20:00 EDT

N. P. Vizarim, C. J. O. Reichhardt, C. Reichhardt

We investigate how the translational motion of a compact skyrmion cluster can be converted into collective rotation through interactions with a periodic obstacle substrate. Using an effective particle model, we consider a seven-skyrmion cluster driven along a one-dimensional array of repulsive obstacle sites. For suitable obstacle spacings and interaction strengths, repeated interactions with the substrate produce coherent clockwise or counterclockwise rotation, giving rise to gear-like cluster dynamics. We characterize these states using the translational velocity, Hall angle, angular velocity, structural deformation, and a dimensionless gear ratio that relates cluster rotation to motion over the substrate periodicity. Dynamical phase diagrams identify rotating, nonrotating, wall-guided, transverse, and deformed regimes. We further show that coherent rotation persists in the overdamped limit, demonstrating that the conversion mechanism does not require an intrinsic Magnus force and may therefore extend to other driven cluster systems, including colloidal assemblies. These results provide a simple route for controlling collective rotation through structured substrates.
